## Anything and everything

When asked what could go wrong with a macroeconomic forecast, the appropriate answer is always “anything and everything.” Our narrative foresees resilient US and global expansions in which growth downshifts to a trend-like pace amid a broadening regional and sectoral base. Meanwhile we expect core inflation to remain sticky above central bank targets. The space for central bank easing has thus been anticipated to be limited. Recent releases challenge each of these views. The most important news is in the US where rising downside growth risks have been accompanied by moderating labor market pressures. Although the FOMC guided toward a gradual policy easing at this week’s meeting, these developments likely interact to shift the Fed’s perception of risk decisively toward labor market weakness. The door is thus opening for a more forceful monetary policy adjustment, and we now look for the Fed to ease 50bp at the next two meetings.

![](images/9cdad173c74844e779372ea14f48c3227cf17c753fa3527add891622fcf634d6.jpg)

In recent weeks, the midyear slide in DM manufacturing surveys and signs of a loss in Euro area momentum were challenging our view of growth broadening out. These concerns are reinforced this week by a sharp decline in the July EM PMIs. Taken together, the global output PMI has dropped 2.5pts in two months, to a level consistent with a stall in global factory output gains. The details of the report point to even greater weakness in new orders (Figure 1). Combined with the rise in the inventory PMI, the report contrasts with our view that solid retail and capex growth will support 2H24 global IP growth close to the 2.1%ar it recorded last quarter.

Although the slowing in US employment gains to 114k last month was disappointing, we temper the weight placed on this slide. A surge in employees out of work due to bad weather alongside a workweek decline points to a Hurricane Beryl drag. The allowance for a modest reversal of this drag next month would align job growth with our trajectory of normalizing toward a still solid pace. However, three related labor market developments raise concern. First, as storms depress hours, incomes are normally smoothed as workers continue to be paid, a development not evident in this week’s employment report. Second, the sectoral breadth of job creation narrowed sharply as the diffusion index slipped to 49.6 last month. This signal, consistent with recent business surveys, is not easily linked to bad weather. Third, we have attributed a rising unemployment rate to labor supply gains and a moderation in labor demand that has increased the duration of job searches (Figure 2). While this month’s unemployment rate rise reflects these trends, a notable increase in the flow of workers from employed to unemployed hints at the start of a more troubling behavioral shift toward job shedding.

It is too early to distinguish between a moderation and break in labor market conditions, but the risks of a break are rising. As the Fed balances its dual mandate, a major shift in the inflation risk profile is taking place. The US unemployment rate has increased 0.9%-pt from last year’s low as an immigration surge and a rise in the prime age participation rate to a 23-year high have rebalanced a labor market still generating strong demand. With this week’s ECI report reinforcing the signal from average hourly earnings on wage moderation, and productivity gains limiting unit labor costs, inflation concerns related to tight labor markets have been allayed.

Together these shifts materially change the balance of risk, warranting a level adjustment in the Fed stance. While an easing at each of the meetings for the rest of this year looks likely, the magnitude of moves depends on the data. While there are two CPI reports before the September 18 meeting, news related to growth will likely play the biggest role.

## Western Europe CBs to remain gradualists

In contrast to this week’s significant shift in our Fed call, the news flow keeps Western European central banks on track for the modest easing we expect through year-end. Key to this difference is a Fed that is responding to a more pronounced shift in the perception of risk. If the US slips into recession, the risk profile for Western Europe would change materially and therefore require a reassessment of our views.

The Bank of England cut rates this week in a finely balanced 5-4 vote—delivering on our long-standing August call. The easing came despite a pickup in growth and ongoing strength in wages and services inflation. We

interpret Governor Bailey’s message, and the BoE’s projections, as consistent with a quarterly pace of easing, in line with our forecast. The BoE did hint that it could cut more quickly if inflation risks dissipate but November is the most likely date for the next ease. Notably, the BoE will update fiscal assumptions after the Budget in late October. The new Labor government this week argued that higher near-term spending obligations necessitated new tax increases. We continue to expect these to be relatively small, however, with Labor also likely to accept cutbacks to future spending.

In the Euro area growth disappointments remain modest while inflation stickiness persists. Regional manufacturing, particularly the German industrial sector, remains depressed, but spillovers to the labor market remained limited. Indeed, this week saw the Euro area unemployment rate rise to a level unchanged from a year ago alongside solid job gains. Meanwhile, core HICP inflation surprised to the upside with a 0.3%m/m July increase. With better news on service price inflation details and the regional-wide composite PMI slipping, the ECB is on course for a 25bp September ease. However, the case for a faster pace of easing will require a shift in the perception of macroeconomic risk.

## BoJ delivers hawkish wake-up call

In hiking its policy rate 15bp this week to 0.25% and announcing a plan to halve its gross monthly bond purchases over the next couple of years, the Bank of Japan sent a hawkish message. Governor Ueda stated that more hikes will be delivered if the data flow comes in as expected and stressed that policy settings will remain accommodative even after a few more rate hikes. This clear hawkish break was reflected in our NLP model, which puts the statement on par with some of the most hawkish on record for the BoJ. In suggesting that a series of hikes would be needed to bring the BoJ closer to neutral, we think that Ueda has signaled in concrete terms that the terminal rate will be higher than the 0.5% level priced by markets over the next 12 months (Figure 3). We expect the BoJ policy rates to reach 1%.

Figure 3: Japan NLP Hawk-Dove Score and market pricing of BoJ
![](images/c71b3583b27e4c714cfd4445aee2fb9d4d70efcf88976bc4bbbae1bbb7861fab.jpg)

The BoJ’s hawkish turn seems to have caught some off-guard, given what has been a patchy run for macro activity over the past several quarters. The BoJ’s outlook suggests that strong wage momentum will support spending and incoming activity data suggest that this recovery is now taking hold. This week’s June retail sales and July consumer sentiment reports were positive, and manufacturers’ projections into 3Q were nudged up. We have upgraded our forecast for real consumption and GDP to grow at a roughly 2%ar in the middle quarters of this year, a development that could be sustained in an environment of supportive external conditions.

## A disappointing start to 3Q in China…

The disappointing news on China’s activity continued this week. The July NBS PMI pointed to further softening in domestic demand, while a large 4.4-pt drop in the Caixin manufacturing output PMI raises concerns that China’s exports, which provided a major lift to 1H growth, may also be losing momentum. Despite mounting pressure for additional policy support, this week’s Politburo meeting did not convey any urgency. There was a greater emphasis on boosting consumer spending, particularly for services, but the 2H policy guidelines were like those from the April meeting. Additional fiscal stimulus remains unlikely after last week’s rate cuts. Instead, policy support should come from accelerating the issuance of special local government bonds and providing more flexibility in the deployment of these funds. We see risks skewed to the downside of our forecast for GDP to advance a modest 4.5%ar in 2H24.

## … but still-solid growth in EMAX

Against the weak outturns across most of the rest of the world, the July manufacturing PMIs remain consistent with solid growth in EM Asia outside Mainland China. The region’s PMIs retraced only slightly from their two-year highs and new export orders held up at a strong level. The ongoing tailwind from tech demand, complemented by a modest recovery in domestic demand, keeps us confident that 3Q GDP growth will pick up to a 3%ar after disappointing in 2Q. This week, Korea’s June IP posted a strong gain in tech production accompanied by a surge in shipments and a sharp correction in inventories. We expect Korean GDP to rebound 4%ar in 3Q from -0.9%ar in 2Q. Disappointment in China’s export engine could still tilt regional growth risks to the downside, especially in economies where the share of exports to China still constitutes more than 20% of all exports (Indonesia, Korea, Taiwan).

## Financial stability risks to keep RBI on hold

We expect the RBI to stand pat at next week’s monetary policy review and reiterate its “withdrawal of accommodation” stance. While food price dynamics should turn more benign as the monsoon improves, the central bank will need to see more evidence that food inflation—which constitutes almost half the basket—is softening durably and pushing headline CPI closer to the 4% inflation target. The RBI will also be mindful of ongoing financial stability risks given the surge in unsecured lending to households over the last 18 months, which has nonetheless started to soften in response to macroprudential measures. We expect the RBI to ease in 4Q as growth softens and inflation moderates.

## CE-3 mirrors Euro area performance

The 2Q GDP releases from Central Europe mirror Euro area performance, a disappointment for a region with a higher underlying potential pace (Figure 4). Czechia and Hungary both disappointed, maintaining the sub-par pace of the previous two quarters. The region’s export-oriented manufacturing sector has strong links with German supply chains and is facing similar problems. Moreover, a recovery in consumer spending has been underwhelming and EU fiscal transfers have yet to deliver an expected boost to capex in this cycle. Further growth disappointment could deliver more than the limited rate cuts we are forecasting. But, for now, policymakers remain focused on still sticky above-target inflation and elevated wage growth (outside Czechia). Poland’s flash July CPI showed core inflation firming to a 4%ar in the three months to July (0.4%m/m, sa in July) while underlying inflation in Hungary is running close to a 5%ar.

![](images/b291820431ff11ea5ee8329ecb4574afbd874857ff56f33078b12d6375a04d46.jpg)

## Idiosyncratic forces on Latam CBs

Three out of the five major Latin American central banks met last Wednesday, before this week’s weak US labor report. Two delivered as expected, with Brazil’s BCB keeping the policy rate at 10.5% and Colombia’s BanRep cutting 50bp. BCB worries about de-anchored inflation expectations and FX depreciation, but remains comfortable with a stable SEL-IC rate, while internal BanRep divisions pointed to some who wanted a 75bp cut. Such developments are consistent with our forecasts for rates of 10.5% for Brazil and 8.5% for Colombia at year-end. Next week, we anticipate a 3-2 split with Mexico’s Banxico delivering a 25bp cut.

## Economic Activity Tracking

See here for a real-time snapshot and methodology reports.

Our estimate for 2Q24 global real GDP growth ticked down 0.1%-pt this week to 1.9%ar (Figure 1), while the top-down global nowcaster for 2Q moved sideways at 2.4%ar. The nowcaster is tracking 3Q growth at 2.0%ar (Table 2).

About 20% of the economies we track have seen data surprise stronger over the past 30 days. The nowcaster implies upside risk to 2Q growth in a little over half of these economies (Figure 2).

Our CapexNow model suggests global capex growth will slow to a stall this quarter after growing 2.8%ar in 2Q24 (Table 4).

The global manufacturing PMI for July suggests a slowdown the pace in factory output at midyear, although solid EM export activity provides some reassurance that the manufacturing sector will remain resilient.

## J.P. Morgan Market Watch

## Bonds

Bonds have rallied this week. US yields finished 10-17bp lower and the curve steepened further amid evidence of weakening in labor markets. Markets are likely to remain focused on the risk that a more abrupt slowing in the labor market could force the Fed to cut rates more aggressively. We see a risk of cuts every meeting if the situation deteriorates further. We remain neutral duration at current levels and continue to recommend 5s/30s steepeners as a low-beta long with better carry ahead of the upcoming easing cycle.

In the Euro area, ECB guidance at the July meeting indicated the need to focus on the broader inflation dynamic and we are still calling for two ECB rate cuts this year in September and December. As a result of the repricing this week, we have taken profit on our tactical longs in 5Y Germany and Dec24/ Dec25 Euribor flattener. In the UK, BoE cut rates 25bps in August, in a finely balanced decision. We hold long Sep24 MPC OIS, 1Y/1Yx1Y SONIA curve flatteners and long 5Yx5Y SONIA vs. SOFR. In Japan, the BoJ hiked the policy rate to 0.25% and announced a QT plan. We recommend tactically entering 10Y swap spread narrowers.

In EM, Brazil inflation has moved close to the BCB’s target but rising inflation expectations have pushed markets to price in rate hikes this year, we see rates remaining on hold. While Mexico sees upside inflation surprises in June and July are challenging Banxico’s earlier dovish pivot. We stay neutral EM local bonds overall. We remain OW India, Poland, Türkiye and Uruguay and UWs in Thailand and Chile (Global Fixed Income Markets Weekly, Aug 2<sup>nd</sup>).

## Credit

US HG had a more active month-end in July as supply had been above average and so had fund inflows, particularly on the passive side. The pace of fund inflows MTD were higher than the YTD pace. The passive inflows MTD were the highest since February 2024. The passive flows tend to lead to more month-end trading activity. The index is expanding by \$19bn par, growing again after a net decline last month as fewer bonds were exiting the month (2025 maturities) while issuance had been above average for July (US HG Month-Ender, Jul 29<sup>th</sup>).

High Yield Issuer Fundamentals - 1Q 2024. Headline net leverage composition-adjusted ticked up by 0.1x in 1Q 2024, driven by both seasonally weak Q1 earnings and rising levels of debt. Median interest coverage fell to just 3.7x, its lowest level since 2015, with 28% of our cohort of issuers reporting ratios below 2.5x. The weakness was once again more pronounced within lower rated credits with the average CCC company only 2.4x covered, while BBs and single-Bs remain comfortably above their 2014/15 lows (High Yield Talking Points, Jul 31<sup>st</sup>).

Figure 1: Year-to-date returns %
![](images/d31cf5e218625923f4805bafca59de002cea3bedc7f47e7c262367d8880ddea7.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan

In Euro credit, we are revising our FY24 euro IG and HY spread targets upwards to 150bp and 425bp, respectively. In our view, the market is facing a potent cocktail of: i) divergence in spreads from rates markets and equity volatility; ii) weaker economic data; iii) more balanced technicals with risks to demand from lower yields; iv) geopolitical risk; and v) an unwinding in stretched segments of broader financial markets (European Credit Weekly, Aug 2<sup>nd</sup>).

## Currencies

Yen-maggedon. The 5%+ intra-month rally in the Yen ranks among the most extreme in two decades. Acute Yen strength on this scale tends to peter out fairly quickly, though old Yen lows are not easily revisited. The convulsion in carry resembles typical risk bearish August seasonality pulled forward. Yen contagion to other funding currencies is most plausible for CHF, and least for CNY.

Trade Recommendations: Neutral on JPY but keep risk-off/ deleveraging exposure by virtue of long-USD & short NZD. Take profit on long AUD/NZD but stay short NZD/USD. Sell EUR/CHF on disappointing EU cyclicals and potential fur-

ther deleveraging of CHF shorts. Short EUR vs USD & NOK. We stay MW in EM FX which is likely held back in 2H24 by US elections, even as macro backdrop has improved with Fed likely to cut in Sept. Favour idiosyncratic carry stories and currencies with attractive valuations (FXMW, Jul 26<sup>th</sup>).

## Commodities

Our fair value model proved to be remarkably stable and projects Brent prices close to \$90 by September 2024 and \$63 by December 2025. The main hurdle to \$90 remains the misplaced doubts about the health of global demand and market’s fixation on a fundamentally weak 2H25. Furthermore, the most important market consequence of a US election that has become more competitive, is a big increase in uncertainty around the outcome. A victory by the incumbent Democratic party is the most market-neutral scenario, while implications of another Trump presidency could be net bearish for oil as the return of a ‘maximum pressure’ campaign on Iran is ultimately undercut by the negative macro impacts of rising trade tariffs. Overall, the market appears to be looking through near-term supportive dynamics, being more wholly fixated by a bearish 2025 (Oil Markets Weekly, Aug 1<sup>st</sup>).

US Natural gas near-term fundamental weakness clouds the 2025 narrative but price upside still apparent. We have revised our 2025 price forecast down to \$3.70/MMBtu from \$4.30/MMBtu to reflect the now more dominating fears of production and a lack of weather demand versus what we view as strong structural demand support from the likes of increasing LNG export capacity and baseload power generation demand. Despite this revision lower, we view risks to our forecast skewed to the upside at realization with the potential for regional tightness (US Natural Gas, Aug 2<sup>nd</sup>). We revise lower our price forecast for the European natural gas balance of 2024, with 3Q24 now expected to average 35 EUR/ MWh and 4Q24 to average 45 EUR/MWh. Our continued bullish view is centered around potential supply disruption risks ranging from upcoming Norwegian maintenance, uncertainties around remaining Russian pipeline supply, and Europe’s need to increase its bid on global LNG as demand in Asia continues to perform (European Natural Gas, Jul 31<sup>st</sup>).

Positioning has cleaned up across base metals after sharp investor de-risking in the last month prompted by slumping PMIs, US election volatility, disappointment following Chinese policy meetings and a loss of patience on fundamentals. While prices have now pulled back lower to attractive levels once again and we expect copper micro fundamentals will continue to improve in the coming months, we think it will still take some time for bullish fundamental catalysts to re-excite broader interest in the complex (Metals Weekly, Aug 2<sup>nd</sup>). Outflows return to commodity markets amid gold led positioning crunch. The estimated value of open interest

across global commodity markets plunged to the lowest level since March, driven by price and contract-flow driven losses across precious and base metals along with crude oil. Despite gold’s resilience in pricing, Managed Money net length in COMEX Gold futures dipped but we maintain a multi-year bullish outlook on gold silver, and boosted year ahead targets to \$2,800/oz & \$36/oz, respectively (Commodity Market Positioning & Flows, Jul 29<sup>th</sup>). Sugar challenged by producer FX, but constructive fundamentals remain intact. Our highest conviction bullish call across agri markets, Sugar has been under considerable macro market pressure through July. We maintain the call that sugar offers value on the long side to consumers and investors alike off these lows (Agricultural Markets Weekly, Jul 26<sup>th</sup>).

## Politics and monetary independence

• The most synchronized policy easing cycle outside of recession is starting at a time of most elections ever

• Political influence on monetary decisions is a concern

The evidence shows little impact of elections on policy deviations from Taylor rule-prescribed actions

• Fed transcripts show evolving concern about political appearances; Resolved by improved transparency

The world is undergoing a rare confluence of unprecedented monetary policy and a historical display of democracy. As noted in a recent special report, the global economy is about to start the most synchronized easing cycle outside of a recession ever seen. At the same time, half the world’s population are heading to the polls this year—with over 64 countries holding national elections. Most of these elections have important implications for fiscal policies.

Figure 1: Global central bank moves around elections Number of times a CB moved in Q or Q-1 of election, 1980-2023
![](images/380b3e3592dc00dbfcb317b507804b4ade44b3c24c831f0e2d8b52da8b4dfaff.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan Global Economics

With such consequential monetary and political events this year, it is natural to consider the whether the latter will impact the former. Operational independence has been a cornerstone of central banking in the DM for some time, e.g. as far back as the 1951 Treasury Accord for the Fed and 1997 for the BoE. For the EM, independence is a somewhat more recent development but one that has been crucial for lifting the region onto more stable ground in the past two decades. Central banks are not immune from taking action in either direction (cuts or hikes) around elections. Indeed, of the 253 elections across 24 economies since 1980, central banks have hiked 98 times, eased 91 times, and remained on hold 72 times (including occasions where central banks both hiked and cut) (Figure 1). Still, one could argue the uncertainties around elections could influence central banks.

Whatever the reasons, politics is always lurking in the background and raising questions about the intermingling of policies. These questions have become more pressing as politics across both the DM and EM alike have become highly charged. In this report, we explore the impact of elections on monetary policy. We begin with a focus on the Fed but then extend this to 21 countries. In all cases, we control for what central banks “should do” by considering whether changes in policy setting ahead of elections deviate from what would be prescribed by the macroeconomic backdrop.

We see little evidence that politics influences the Fed. Policy rate changes in the two quarters ahead of an election do not deviate from their Taylor rule-prescribed path in a way that is statistically different from non-election periods. Nor is the response of the fed funds rate to macro conditions (as indicated by the Taylor rule) altered by elections. When extended to the rest of the world, we come to the same conclusion although with a few cases of marginal significance. However, these results are not compelling. We conclude that electoral influence on monetary policy is not material across the world.

We also provide a deep dive into the transcripts from FOMC meetings. We identify several examples back in the past four decades where politics is discussed ahead of elections. Indeed, concerns around politics seem to come up more often prior to the introduction of more thorough communication policies. In all cases, however, the examples highlight the need to do what is consistent with the demands of the Fed’s dual mandate and a desire to avoid the appearance of political influence. There is discussion of the impact of political uncertainty on business sentiment and its impact on the economy. This sentiment channel of politics is not surprising and something we have highlighted in other research. There is nothing in our results that would lead us to believe politics will deter a rate cut at the upcoming FOMC meeting in September should the committee deem it appropriate, a view echoed by Chair Powell at this week’s post-meeting press conference. Of course, a September rate cut would be unique in that it would be the first time a cutting cycle began just two months before an election.

## Fed behavior and elections: A framework

With inflation showing signs of cooling in the US, the Fed inched closer this week to beginning its easing cycle. Despite a consensus at the start of the year questioning whether the Fed could begin a “consequential” first cut (as Chair Powell has called it) just two months ahead of an equally consequential election, the Fed seems to be guiding toward just that. The proximity of a potential Fed action to the November 5 election has raised questions around Fed independence. This has been amplified by recent rhetoric from the Republican candidate Trump and reports of proposals by Trump allies to erode Fed independence. These concerns raise the specter of the lead up to the 1972 election, when audio tapes show President Richard Nixon demanded Arthur Burns supply an expansionary monetary policy and a growing economy.

Chair Powell has emphasized that political considerations do not factor into Fed decisions. Since 1960, the Fed has hiked rates seven times within six months of an election and cut rates seven times (including four times when it has both hiked and cut), while remaining on hold six times. In the modern era, the record is equally mixed since the mid-1980s (Figure 2). This highlights a Fed willing to act as it sees appropriate rather than systematically moving ahead of elections. Cuts preceded the 1984, 1992, and 2008 elections while rate hikes were seen heading into 2004 elections.

Figure 2: Fed funds rate around US elections, 1984-2016 %-pt p.a., deviation from November (1996, 2012 not shown, no change)
![](images/462cb2d7674db78243b520fec2ea977be2d73f89dd221f663c08e9209293a2da.jpg)

Fed action or inaction around elections needs to be assessed relative to what is warranted. To account for this, we look at the textbook balanced Taylor rule that was shown in a recent special report to explain much of current central bank behavior around the world. In Figure 3, we show the %-point change in the fed funds rate in the six months through October of every year since 1960. The horizontal axis shows the Taylor rule change and the vertical axis shows the actual change.

Figure 3: Fed funds rate change, actual and Taylor %-pt chg in Fed funds rate, thru 6 months to Oct. (Sample: Annual 1960-2020)
![](images/b81b383980c9017c266c45f2c71503bae8ac54aef5c8e74b9a687893d1143d86.jpg)

We see little graphical evidence of systematic Taylor deviations in policy setting around US elections. Policy rate changes align with the Taylor rule as expected, with the fed funds rate moving 50bp for every 100bp move in the Taylor rule.

The blue dots in Figure 3 highlight election years. Only a few elections deviate in a meaningful way. In the six months ahead of the 1980 election, rates were hiked aggressively despite macro conditions pointing to no change. However, given the aggressive attempts to re-anchor inflation expectations after the 1970s inflation surge, 1980 is not a useful comparison. It is notable that politics were not discussed in the 1980 FOMC transcripts. The deviation in 2008 was fleeting as the Fed was rapidly cutting and moved to the zero lower bound just after the election. The significant deviation in 2020 is also not useful given the zero lower bound during the pandemic. Beyond these extremes, policy around elections seems to fall within the range of what would have been expected.

## Limited influence around the world

Over half the world’s population is going to the polls in 2024—the most ever seen. At the same time, central banks everywhere are looking to move restrictive stances back toward neutral. To explore the influence of politics on monetary policy, we apply a regression analysis to 24 economies over a range of years (with a total of 642 country-years). Similar to the graphical analysis for the US, we control for the underlying macro conditions by computing the balanced Taylor rule for each economy. We regress the two-quarter change in the policy rate on the two-quarter change in the Taylor rule. We also include a dummy variable for whether the quarter has an election. If statistically significant, this implies that policy over the two quarters ahead of an election is systematically different than what would be warranted by macro conditions.

Results are reported in Table 1. In nearly all cases, the Taylor rule is statistically significant. This is consistent with the evidence reported in our recent special report exploring the role of macro conditions in explaining monetary policy. The coefficients are meaningfully below one however—a reminder that the textbook Taylor rule is useful but not perfect. In almost all cases, policy does not appear to systematically deviate from its Taylor rule-prescribed path around elections. The exceptions are the ECB (where we are looking at European Parliamentary elections), Korea, and South Africa. We do not find these particularly compelling cases however, with either small coefficients or large standard errors.

We also explore whether elections influence the degree to which policy responds to macro conditions. Specifically, we estimate the impact of elections on the transmission of macro conditions through the Taylor rule onto policy changes. This is done by interacting the election dummy variable with the change in the Taylor rule. The results are reported in Table 2, and yield a similar conclusion: very little evidence of material election influence on policy setting. Again, there are some exceptions. Notably, elections seem to damp the policy response to changes in the Taylor rule in the UK, Australia, and Colombia, while boosting it in Mexico. As with the first analysis, we do not find these compelling cases and stand by the conclusion that elections do not have significant influence on monetary policy setting.

## Transcripts show importance of clarity

The statistical evidence above supports what Chair Powell stated in April:

“…Congress granted the Fed a substantial degree of independence in our conduct of monetary policy…This independence both enables and requires us to make our monetary policy decisions without consideration of short-term political matters… independence is essential to our ability to serve the public.”

Chair Powell went on to say at the May press conference that one can easily examine the FOMC transcripts if there is any doubt about the veracity of his comments on this matter:

“There’s a significant difference between an institution that takes into account all sorts of political events and one that doesn’t. That’s where the significant difference is, and, you know, we’re—we just don’t do that. I mean, you can go back and read the transcripts for every—this is my fourth election, fourth presidential election here. Read all the transcripts, and see if anybody mentions, in any way, the pending election. It just isn’t part of our thinking. It’s not what we’re hired to do. If we start down that road, again, I don’t know how you stop.”

We have taken Powell’s suggestion and checked the transcripts of every presidential election year back to 1980 (through the latest available in 2016). We view this exploration as indicative not only of Fed policy, but as likely reflective of most central bank thinking when it comes to politics and monetary policy. The record confirms the spirit of Powell’s sentiments expressed above even if not the letter. Specifically, we see a number of takeaways from our reading:

Politics is discussed and more so in the early years. In contrast to Powell’s claim, politics do come up at FOMC meetings and in some cases with direct reference to the influence on policy setting. We see politics and the election mentioned over a dozen times across the 10 elections from 1980 to 2016, with only 3 elections garnering no mention (1980, 1988, 1996). Political considerations stemming from the election, while not impacting decisions, seemed more at top of mind in the earlier years. At the May 1984 FOMC meeting, Vice Chair Martin stated policy action “is called for both for economic and political considerations.” Perhaps the most telling conversation on politics is found at the October 1992 FOMC meeting when Dallas Fed president McTeer stated “I thought it was conventional wisdom that we weren't expected to act so close to an election,” and went on to claim that “tightening doesn't affect our credibility the same way that easing might.” Rather than disabuse McTeer of that note, Chair Greenspan later stated “I wish we had the luxury to sit back and do nothing until after the election, as is the conventional procedure of the Federal Open Market Committee.” A similar sentiment was expressed as recently as the September 2000 FOMC meeting when Richmond Fed president Broaddus stated “I would hate to wait until late in August, a couple of months before an election, and find ourselves in a real bind.” And in June 2008, Dallas Fed president Fisher stated that “I am not sure that things will get easier by August. We then get into an election season, which I think still conditions somewhat our thinking.”

More recent statement of principle to do what is right. More aligned with Powell’s sentiment is the degree to which all discussions start from the standpoint of wanting to enact the appropriate policies consistent with the mandate. At the November 2016 FOMC meeting just ahead of the election, Vice Chair Fischer noted “Are we, therefore, going to be making decisions that reflect our political preferences? Absolutely not… we will be making decisions that the Committee believes, on the basis of its professional judgments, are in the best interests of the United States.” This principle was echoed by Governor Olson from the September 2004 FOMC meeting, “The question to my mind was never whether the FOMC would allow political pressure to interfere with its decisions on the appropriate monetary policy—I was certain it wouldn’t.”

Appearances are top of mind. How politics “conditioned” the Fed’s thinking is not clear. However, a key concern of the Fed looks to be about the appearance of undue influence. This concern has gone as far as to actually impact at least one decision, albeit not a rate decision. At the December 2016 meeting, Vice Chair Dudley noted he had intended to want a fresh rewrite of the policy statement but that “I changed my mind about that after the election, because of concerns that a major rewrite might be misconstrued as motivated by the election outcome.” Dudley’s concern about appearances were also seen at the September 2012 meeting, at which he noted a failure to act “might signal that we were cowed by the political environment, which I think would be very unfortunate.” These concerns about appearance arguably had some influence on decisions, at least in the earlier years. At the July 1992 FOMC meeting Governor Kelley stated the Fed would do “serious long-term damage if we were to ease and if, however inaccurately, the market perceived that we were pandering to politics.” At the same time, likely reflecting the views of the more modern Fed, Governor Olsen took the opposite stance in September 2004 in stating that “the general sense of the appropriate implementation of our policy ensures our credibility.”

Impact of political uncertainty always a concern. While the direct impact of politics on decisions making is a hot button issue, the impact of political uncertainty on business and household behavior is almost always a topic of discussion. This complicates assessing the appropriateness political considerations in policy setting. In September 2012, Governor Raskin worried: “I project consumption to be damped this year through a worsening of consumer confidence as the presidential election heats up…So even if the data are improving, that may be nearly irrelevant to people’s perception that the economic recovery is subpar.” These concerns can go as far as to indirectly influence policy setting. Despite clearly stating his assessment for a rate hike in September 2016, Philadelphia Fed President Harker noted “due to a variety of risks, including heightened political and subsequent policy uncertainty, I can see this case for being a bit cautious.”

Communication and transparency are key. The underlying debate within the transcripts over doing or not doing the right thing in order to ensure the appearance of propriety is resolved through transparency. It is not surprising that the shift to clearer communications from the Fed over the past three decades has greatly improved its transmission. It has also meant the concerns around political influence have faded. In November 1992, Philadelphia Fed president Boehne went as far as to suggest the FOMC could “write a letter” explaining their actions to avoid the appearance of influence. A lot has changed since then. Policy statements, forecasts, and post-meeting press conferences are all modern tools that shine light on the reasoning behind policy decisions. In this regard, Governor Olsen refers in September 2004 to the Fed’s “clarity of communication” for explaining the FOMC’s policy actions independent of political considerations.

In general, independence and transparency go hand in hand, reinforcing each other. Independence has been a bedrock of monetary policy for decades, and more recently for many EM central banks. Transparency has been on the rise for all. This has proved a powerful addition to the monetary policy toolkit. As noted in Fed transcripts, the “clarity” of communications has cut the Gordian knot tying the concerns over the appearance of impropriety to political considerations in policy setting.

It is telling that a key proponent of this “clarity”, Ben Bernanke, shot back quickly at Dallas Fed president Fisher’s June 2008 meeting comments about political considerations (noted above): “President Fisher, I think I have to note for the record that I don’t think we should let political considerations affect our decisions in any way, and I am not concerned about that.” Echoing this sentiment eight year later, Vice Chair Fischer extolled in November 2016: “I’m confident we’ll continue to follow that principle, as is both our legal obligation and our standard practice.” The same can likely be said for most central banks.

## Appendix: Fed transcripts

Below are excerpts from FOMC transcripts in presidential election years from1980 to 2016 (the most recent available). Transcripts are searched for “politic” (yielding “political”, “politics”, “politician”, etc.), “election”, and “November”. Relevant quotes were identified for inclusion. This does not include every reference made regarding economic impacts stemming from elections, which are numerous.

## November 2016 Election

FOMC Meeting: September 2016

“Because of my fairly upbeat outlook for the economy and the slow but persistent march of inflation to target, I could easily support a funds rate increase at this meeting. However, due to a variety of risks, including heightened political and subsequent policy uncertainty, I can see this case for being a bit cautious.”

\~ Patrick Harker, Philadelphia Fed “MR. FISCHER. For those who are going to be here four years from now, is it essential to set the meeting just before the election? VICE CHAIRMAN DUDLEY. You mean maybe moving it up a week? MR. MADIGAN. We look at a lot of events and constraints in constructing the calendar. VICE CHAIRMAN DUDLEY. The BIS, I think, is a constraint there, because the BIS meeting typically is going to be right in front of the election. So I think the BIS is a constraint there, Stan. MR. LACKER. It does demonstrate our nonchalance. [Laughter] VICE CHAIRMAN DUDLEY. I don’t think it’s a big deal, myself. CHAIR YELLEN. Okay.”

\~ Stanley Fischer, Vice Chair

\~ William Dudley, New York Fed

\~ Brian Madigan, FOMC Secretary

\~ Jeffrey Lacker, Richmond Fed

\~ Janet Yellen, Chair

FOMC voted to maintain target range, 7-3 decision with George, Mester, and Rosengren dissenting for 25bp increase

FOMC Meeting: November 2016

“So the lack of urgency implies that there is not a good case for moving at this meeting. To do so with the election a week away, the outcome uncertain, and no scheduled press conference would imply an urgency to move that I just don’t think is consistent with the incoming information or the economic outlook.”

\~William Dudley, New York Fed

“Finally, as we look to our December decision, we will be navigating treacherous waters. When we make our decision in December, we should be looking ahead and choosing an interest rate that reflects our expectations of future economic policies and of the future state of the economy. Those expectations will and should reflect our expectations of the nonmonetary economic policies that will be followed in 2017 and later. Are we, therefore, going to be making decisions that reflect our political preferences? Absolutely not. As the Chair has emphasized, we will be making decisions that the Committee believes, on the basis of its professional judgments, are in the best interests of the United States. I’m confident we’ll continue to follow that principle, as is both our legal obligation and our standard practice.”

\~ Stanley Fischer, Vice Chair

FOMC voted to maintain target range, 8-2 decision with George and Mester dissenting for 25bp increase

FOMC Meeting: December 2016

“A few months ago, I thought that our first rate hike in a year might be an opportunity for a major “refresh” of the state-

ment. But I changed my mind about that after the election, because of concerns that a major rewrite might be misconstrued as motivated by the election outcome or taken as a major shift in policy direction. We don’t want the statement to be viewed in that way, so I think a statement with minimal changes, except for the decision to raise the federal funds rate target, seems appropriate to me… The minimal changes in the statement language should be reassuring to people that the election outcome has not yet provoked a radical rethink of the policy outlook.”

\~ William Dudley, New York Fed

FOMC voted to raise target range by 25bps, unanimous decision

## November 2012 Election FOMC Meeting: April 2012

“Fourth, I project consumption to be damped this year through a worsening of consumer confidence as the presidential election heats up. It may feel as if the presidential debate is about dogs riding on car roofs. No, the focus of this election year is the economy, and extensive campaigning is likely to accentuate discontent with the economic recovery. So even if the data are improving, that may be nearly irrelevant to people’s perception that the economic recovery is subpar. This perception could show up in lackluster confidence and consumer spending.”

\~ Sarah Bloom Raskin, Governor

FOMC voted to maintain policy rate, 9-1 decision with Lacker dissenting against wording of statement

FOMC Meeting: September 2012

“I think a failure to act forcefully at this meeting would be very damaging to confidence; it would imply that we’re out of ammunition or almost out of ammunition; and it also might signal that we were cowed by the political environment, which I think would be very unfortunate.”

\~ William Dudley, New York Fed

FOMC voted to increase policy accommodation by purchasing additional agency mortgage-backed securities at a pace of \$40 billion per month, 11-1 decision with Lacker dissenting

## November 2008 Election FOMC Meeting: June 2008

“Yesterday I said I thought there were three ways to deal with our current predicament now that the tail risks have shifted. One is to hope it will cure itself. I don’t believe that is going to happen because of the way micro operators operate. The second is to treat it rhetorically, and I think that the language in alternative B is quite good, if that is the way you wish to go. But I don’t think it is going to get any easier, as was mentioned earlier at this table. I am not sure that things will get easier by August. We then get into an election season, which I think still conditions somewhat our thinking.”

\~ Richard Fisher, Dallas Fed

Bernanke later pushed back:

“President Fisher, I think I have to note for the record that I don’t think we should let political considerations affect our decisions in any way, and I am not concerned about that. I think we are all prepared to do what is necessary. I don’t know what we are going to do in the next meeting or the meeting after that. But my expectation now is that, as others have mentioned, we will begin normalizing interest rates relatively soon, and we should, if possible, begin to prepare the markets for that.”

\~ Ben Bernanke, Chair of Board of Governors

FOMC voted to maintain policy rate target, 9-1 with Fisher dissenting for a 25bp increase

## November 2004 Election FOMC Meeting: September 2004

“But, importantly, monetary policy has not been a political issue. For the three years that I’ve been either on this Committee or thinking about being on this Committee, I’ve wondered about the extent that it would be as we moved into this time frame and came into this meeting. The question to my mind was never whether the FOMC would allow political pressure to interfere with its decisions on the appropriate monetary policy—I was certain it wouldn’t—but the opposite. The question was whether the implementation of appropriate monetary policy would become a political issue.

Mind you, today’s statement will be examined very carefully for political advantage, and attempts to gain that advantage will be made. But almost certainly there will be none, I think for two reasons. First, the general sense of the appropriate implementation of our policy ensures our credibility, and second, there is the clarity of our communication. Now remember, the communication can work only if the first part is accurate. During the last several years, we have gone from being accommodative, to being accommodative for a considerable period, to a time of patience, to removing accommodation at a measured pace. And moving from accommodation toward a return to neutral at a measured pace is not only the appropriate policy, but it will be, in my view, not at all a political issue.”

\~ Mark Olson, Governor

Voted to raise target range by 25bps, unanimous decision

## November 2000 Election

FOMC Meeting: June 2000

“If some question arises, I think it would be particularly appropriate in this intermeeting period to have a call, maybe at the end of July or early August to take another look at the situation. I would hate to wait until late in August, a couple of months before an election, and find ourselves in a real bind. So I make that suggestion.”

\~ J. Alfred Broaddus, Richmond Fed

FOMC voted to maintain federal funds rate target, unanimous decision

## November 1996 Election

Nothing of note in the 1996 transcripts.

## November 1992 Election

FOMC Meeting: February 1992

“In the next couple of weeks there may be a window before Congress starts doing all kinds of things. If there is a desire generally to ease, from a political perspective--though I hate to say it--it may be better to do it sooner than later. On the one hand, it's not expected before Congress gets going, and it may well strengthen the hand of those who wish to try to address the deficit situation. So, if we're thinking in terms of ease, I'd be inclined to urge that it be sooner rather than later.”

\~ Susan Phillips, Governor

“I do think, and Governor Phillips raised this point yesterday, that we may be in a situation--I don't want to use the term "window"--where it's easier for us to make a change now than it may be later as we come closer to a season that may be seen as more political and also as we get into the midst of all this fiscal policy business.”

\~ Richard Syron, Boston Fed

FOMC voted for a directive that called for maintaining the existing degree of pressure on reserve positions, with some bias toward possible easing during the intermeeting period, unanimous decision

FOMC Meeting: July 1992

“Our credibility may be on the line right here. We could do ourselves some serious long-term damage if we were to ease and if, however inaccurately, the market perceived that we were pandering to politics or that we were putting a premium on growth at any cost even if we had to accept a devaluation of the currency and the financial system to get it. That could be very unfortunate. I think we might be in a very important time right now in terms of testing our credibility. If it's very clear that the wheels are coming off the economy and that things are going south, then I think we probably would have to ease and the markets would understand that and would not fault us for it long term. But if an easing is

seen as some kind of panic or a response to political pressure or what people would perceive as mistaken priorities, then I think our credibility would have had it for a long time.”

\~ Edward Kelley Jr., Governor

FOMC voted to cut discount rate 50bp, 10-2 decision with LaWare and Melzer dissenting against bias toward easing

FOMC Meeting: October 1992

“MR. MCTEER. … Yet we're within a month of the election and I thought it was conventional wisdom that we weren't expected to act so close to an election. But I never see any references to that anymore. Whatever happened to that? MR. PRELL. I've seen a few, but I think the more predominant notion is that the recent weakness in economic indicators just seems to make a compelling case in light of the past patterns of behavior of the Committee. MR. BOEHNE. It also isn't true. There are numerous examples of this Committee tightening within a month of an election, not just easing. MR. SYRON. And given lags. MR. MCTEER. Well, tightening doesn't affect our credibility the same way that easing might.”

\~ Robert McTeer Jr., Dallas Fed

\~ Michael Prell, Economist

\~ Edward Boehne, Philadelphia Fed

\~ Robert McTeer Jr., Dallas Fed

“I would like to say finally that I wish we had the luxury to sit back and do nothing until after the election, as is the conventional procedure of the Federal Open Market Committee. I don't think we have that luxury.”

\~ Alan Greenspan, Chair

FOMC voted to maintain unchanged reserve conditions for the present while biasing the directive strongly toward possible easing during the intermeeting period, 8-4 decision with Jordan and Linsey dissenting for immediate action to increase availability of bank reserves, LaWare and Melzer dissenting against easing bias

FOMC Meeting: November 1992

“As far as the timing is concerned, it would look unnecessarily provocative if we did this now unless we were also to ease our short-term [policy] just a little and couple the two actions. I would prefer to wait until February to do it. I think we would experience less political flak if we did that.”

\~ Robert Black, Richmond Fed

“We've done a study, we have some good reasons, and we can explain our action in a letter. In the letter I would emphasize the technical aspects; I would say we believe that this is a technical adjustment and that the 1-1/2 to 5-1/2 percent range is consistent with growth without giving up on the inflation [goals]. There is no foolproof way to do this; there are risks in doing it. But I suspect that the political fallout would be less if we did it now rather than wait.

## \~ Edward Boehne, Philadelphia Fed

“There are a lot of political judgments with regard to the timing. Some might view it as a bit sneaky to do it after the election with Congress out of town. It might be more orderly to do it in February. If we do it in February, it will come in the face of the new Administration and its proposals on fiscal policy and might be viewed as a signal that we plan to offset those. But my overall view is that this is a long-term range and a long-term strategy and it ought to be done above the fray.”

## \~ David Mullins, Vice Chair

FOMC voted to maintain the existing degree of pressure on reserve positions and include some bias toward possible easing during the intermeeting period, 9-3 decision with LaWare and Melzer dissenting against easing bias, Jordan dissenting for immediate action to increase availability of bank reserves

## November 1988 Election

Nothing of note in the 1988 transcripts.

## November 1984 Election

FOMC Meeting: May 1984

“And, in order to add an element to the discussion that we have all been a little too polite to enunciate, I will say that we are in such an intense political environment at the moment, with so much scrutiny from the Hill and elsewhere about what we are alleged to be doing, that holding to the course--if that's what alternative B with \$1 billion in borrowing and \$600 million in excess reserves means--is called for both for economic and political considerations.”

\~ Preston Martin, Vice Chair

Later: “The political environment is going to get even tenser later in the year.”

## FOMC Meeting: October 1984

Unless there are very clear economic reasons to do otherwise, we ought to continue to be fairly cautious rather than move toward a pronounced easing.”

\~ Anthony Solomon, New York Fed

FOMC voted to ease monetary policy, 9-3 decision with Martin, Rice, and Seger dissenting in favor of further easing

## Australia: Class dismissed

• Student VISA approvals are trending lower, reflecting government policy tightening

• Net inflows of students should follow, consistent with Treasury forecasts for slower net migration

• Monetary policy implications should not be significant...

• ... but given the employment goal, moderating labour supply makes RBA’s “narrow path” a bit tighter still

A few months ago we outlined the implications of slower net migration for the Australian labour market. At that time, student VISA approvals had already started to ease. Approaching the May Budget, the Australian government then proposed caps on places for foreign students in higher education and vocational training courses. Following pushback from these sectors, and with few specifics on student numbers forthcoming, the magnitude of the changes had been unclear. Recent data however suggest stringent settings have indeed been enacted, such that the number of foreign students onshore should ease further over time, in turn helping to lower net overseas migration.

Here we step through what the data show, and implications for policy. The impact of the tightening is already visible in student VISA approvals, but hasn’t hit the ‘hard’ macro data in the sense of arrivals (gross or net), so shouldn’t have impacted economic activity yet. In principle, fewer student placements should be a drag on GDP, through reduced education exports, and loss of broader economic activity by those who would otherwise be residing in the country. The marginal effect should also slow the housing market, and be a negative for labour supply in some sectors. The impact for monetary policy will depend on the net of these influences, and we don’t expect it will be of first order importance. But it does at the margin make it slightly more difficult for the RBA to both hit the inflation target and “keep employment growing”, which is their layman’s phrasing of the dual mandate.

## Expecting slower immigration

Foreign arrivals and net overseas (im)migration (NOM) boomed in the pandemic recovery. The resultant pressure on housing and other fixed infrastructure drew pressure on the government to curtail inflows, and in the May Budget, the government forecasted a normalization. While still a strong number relative to history, the Treasury’s projection of +260K for NOM in FY25 implied a significant easing from FY24 (tracking: +395K) and particularly, FY23’s record pace (+518K, Figure 1).

Figure 1: Net overseas migration 000s, year-ended
![](images/e87133c72cffe963455564f61360d92fcfdcf453d42e4823222e4c5865ebbe41.jpg)

## Student VISA approvals down

As discussed below, the government has less short-term control than Treasury forecasts might imply, though student inflows are one of the most prominent streams, so tightening in this area appeared the most likely channel to slow NOM over time. Government officials had also flagged more stringent VISA tests (e.g., on language requirements) and released a draft framework paper earlier in the year outlining plans to limit foreign student enrolments at an institution level.

Figure 2: Temporary student VISA approval rate on lodgements % of lodgements approved
![](images/2be71d724439943831e548f893cecbf0b650c9a30ef45c6d354a3803758e9d8a.jpg)

Details on the magnitude of these changes were relatively scarce in the May Budget, which noted only that controls would be undertaken “in consultation” with providers in the education sector. More recent data though suggest conditions have indeed tightened. Both grants and lodgements for all student VISAs have fallen and most notably, approval rates are considerably lower than average (Figure 2). The slowing is evident in both higher education, and vocational training streams (Figure 3).

Figure 3: Temporary student VISAs granted (primary, main streams) 000s
![](images/2798fc423dec45b6f846904f09ea68644524ce11f8dfcc749a05d05fa6112275.jpg)

## Foreign student commencements not affected yet...

Students represent around a quarter of temporary VISAs held, and temporary VISA holders represent around 75% of overseas migrant arrival flows represented in Figure 1. However, some approvals are granted to individuals already in the country, so it is not automatic that tighter grant conditions will result in fewer arrivals immediately. Some of the impact could also play out by temporary residents leaving, as their previous VISA status lapses. Both have the same impact on NOM over time.

Figure 4: International student enrollments
![](images/d2659f6bfc1fe53bfd5abe8614ff7eace258bc3a3fd7e0a6176de99dea1b1b46.jpg)

Consistent with this, the reduced flow of grants has not broadly hit international student enrollments, or commencements yet. Figure 4 presents enrollments for a range of education types, with most either up in year on year terms, or flat. Flat is still a strong outcome on a YTD basis, given the prior year’s record strength. Only ELICOS (English language intensive courses) have seen a non-linear change, which reconciles with testing requirements for temporary VISAs having been tightened.

## ...nor are gross/net arrivals...

Higher frequency data on arrivals of permanent and long-term visitors (categories in which those with student VISAs are most likely to appear) have also not dropped yet from the highs, once seasonality is taken into account (Figure 5).

Figure 5: Permanent and long-term arrivals
![](images/169531f181b03d90cd18822b9ced400aa8a2f68575f3c91e9dabfa3ccd864073.jpg)

Given the enrollments data, this suggests the decline in approvals will flow through to arrivals next year, or perhaps, will manifest partly in a rise in departures. So far, both departures and net arrivals are also steady, so don’t yet show any net outflow (Figure 6). The stock of onshore, temporary VISA holders is also unchanged (not shown), though these data are more lagged, and only reported quarterly.

Figure 6: Net permanent and long term arrivals 12 month rolling sum (000s)
![](images/a3d9a881eaf55570cf8084ad09f20f83602b1f891ab4bc4ca68c0b1c683e6fbb.jpg)

## The illusion of control

Though the government has shown a desire to slow student net inflows, the level of NOM is not a choice variable for policymakers. Most obviously, the government can’t perfectly control the number of departures (particularly of local citizens), an outflow which has been unusually low in the pandemic recovery years, so boosting the average pace of NOM.

Figure 7: Temporary VISA holders, larger categories nsa, mn
![](images/104961d1b996b4681a83d8a2e452ccacb9c58dd9a9264f533c95c0f31aa9c088.jpg)

The government also has less ability to influence the inflow of New Zealand citizens, given special arrangements between the two countries (reflected in the special category 444 VISA, Figure 7). Economic conditions have soured in NZ, and given the permissive VISA arrangements between the two countries, the net population flow has tended to reflect relative economic performance, particularly with respect to employment prospects. Not surprisingly, such flows have in the past also been roughly correlated with the AUD/NZD exchange rate (Figure 8), though that relationship has been less reliable in recent years.

Figure 8: Estimated net Aus-NZ immigration and AUD/NZD FX
![](images/6e5c3aa9f3739864ba7710a098a204a60b99a353476c8072089813a8f10f3d48.jpg)

## Fewer people, less GDP

Given the above offsets, the impact of reduced foreign students on the overall economy is likely to be somewhat muted by other factors, which slow the descent in NOM. Still, we expect some impact, which will be felt through a reduction in real and potential GDP, a slower housing market, and relatively weaker labour supply in some sectors.

Individuals are allowed to work up to 48 hours a fortnight on a student VISA when study is in session, and typically fill part-time slots at the lower end of the wage/skill distribution. If the student population is likely to shrink in relative terms, we should expect a relative tightening of labour supply in such sectors (e.g., hospitality and retail), and slower labour supply growth in general (see the first link for more on aggregate participation).

## Weaker labour supply foremost a margin issue

Still, we don’t expect the above factors to have a first order effect on monetary policy. Slower growth is dovish, but is partly reflecting a reduction in labour supply and potential growth, so is ex ante ambiguous for the output gap.

Changes in immigration should have the greatest impact on relative prices, rather than broad inflation. For example, the demand side of the equation should be dominant in sectors with a fixed supply curve and relatively less labour input: housing is the classic example, where reduced net inflow of people should take a little heat out of rents and prices. Rental inflation has been strong, though is not a significant part of the CPI basket to start with (7%), so is unlikely to be a delta for monetary policy. Home-building costs are a larger share of the CPI, which broadens out the potential impact a bit.

Figure 9: Industry concentration and profit margins Herfindahl-Hirschman Index
![](images/c09b1172be7519a4bb606002acdac9b8b0173c5a81ae2f2ac5a06b2c43c07319.jpg)

By contrast, slower immigration can cut in the other direction for more labour-intensive sectors where supply effects potentially overwhelm demand, e,g., in the services sectors mentioned above. Even in this case though, we still expect the impact to be more in margins than prices, as such sectors tend to have little pricing power. The cluster of immigration-reliant sectors lie near the origin of Figure 9: those with relatively low industry concentration (as measured by the Herfindahl-Hirschman Index) and so, relatively low pricing power and thin margins. With GDP growth having already slowed, weaker labour supply is more likely to exacerbate the margin compression which tends to occur at this time of the cycle for such sectors, and in our view is more likely to drag on profits and then employment, than create significant feedback loops to inflation.

## Employment mandate less of a free lunch

Slower growth in labour supply is a potential challenge to the RBA’s messaging however. The external review of the RBA argued the leadership should communicate in a more straightforward manner, and in that service Governor Bullock has used a layman’s depiction of the dual mandate: “hit the inflation target but keep employment growing”. The wording of the latter is convenient, as positive employment growth is relatively easy to achieve – without being definitive either way for the inflation target – when working age population growth is >2%. That threshold of positive employment growth is slightly more likely to induce trade-offs when labour supply isn’t expanding as strongly.

## The unbearable lightness of China’s nominal GDP

• Deflationary pressures in China have become entrenched and pervasive

This has weakened nominal GDP growth markedly, even with real GDP growth managed at around 5%

Deflation reflects policy bias to support production, even at the cost of expanding excess capacity

Low nominal GDP growth is pernicious for corporate earnings, government finances, household income, and debt metrics

Without a redirection of policies, we think nominal GDP growth will slow further

Since the post pandemic reopening in November 2022, attention has been focussed on China’s real GDP growth, which has disappointed. The 2Q24 stumble is the latest reminder of the downside risks. While a 2H recovery is on the cards, we have downgraded our full year growth forecast to 4.7%, below the government’s target of 5%. Several forces are weighing on growth, the two dominant ones being the continued slowdown in housing and weak consumption. Both are the fallout of policy choices: a housing policy that has dampened price correction and exacerbated the decline in transactions and real-estate investment, and a policy stance that has favored production over consumption. While emergent manufacturing sectors have grown rapidly, it has not been sufficient to offset the drags from housing and consumption.

We do not see these policies changing soon, hence our baseline forecast looks for further slowing in growth over the next few years. However, as demonstrated in the last two years, when activity has faltered the authorities tend to increase policy support to keep GDP growth close to the target range. This is becoming increasingly hard, progressively requiring more policy stimulus to generate the same growth impact. A change in US policies post the November election could generate further headwinds via tariffs, and slow the export growth that has buoyed China’s economy in 1H24. But, in our view, there is still policy space to keep real growth close to China’s tolerance level over the next few years, allowing more time for the economy’s dominant growth driver to fully transition from real-estate to emergent manufacturing and for the housing market to stabilize.

The immediate concern, however, is the decline in nominal GDP growth. China’s GDP deflator has declined for the last five consecutive quarters with nominal GDP growth now at its lowest in more than 20 years (barring the pandemic) and below the real growth rate since 2Q23 (Figure 1).

![](images/7b0dabfb18c26caa0b0fc59d78ad3331bdc779058bb7fc00c2f9bf6f1d682185.jpg)

## Deflation comes to China

China’s inflation dynamics have contrasted with those in the rest of the world (Figure 2). The pandemic and lockdowns resulted in unprecedented large negative demand and supply shocks simultaneously. The net result was a sharp drop in growth but a much shallower fall in prices (the collapse in supply cushioning the price impact of the crash in demand). While there were some timing differences, the world, excluding China, reopened together in 1H21. This led to a large and synchronized upswing in global demand. But supply chains that had been disrupted in the pandemic took much longer to repair , especially as China - the world’s manufacturing hub - remained under rolling lockdowns. The spike in inflation was then exacerbated by the Ukraine War.

Figure 2: Global and China CPI inflation
![](images/e8b0c348b065cf126f66c09cc9150b11dfe289897a0e02d953d112c408b994b7.jpg)

From 2H22 , global inflation began to dissipate as global supply chains and energy markets normalized. With the reopening of China in late 2022, supply chain normalization gathered speed and the combined energy and goods disinflation delivered a strong decline in inflation across the world. Services inflation has remained stubbornly high and the goods disinflation now appears to have come to end, turning overall inflation sticky and creating the proverbial “last-mile” problem for central banks. But that’s a different story (link).

China’s inflation experience, however, has been starkly different. While inflation picked up modestly in the extended lockdown, after the reopening it has languished. Since 1Q23,

CPI inflation has averaged 0.2%oya, compared to the 2%oya pre-pandemic (2015-19) average. This reflects contrasting demand and supply shocks. The reopening surge in demand ended quickly and demand growth has languished since 2Q23. Supply has recovered strongly aided by China’s lopsided policy support that has favored production over consumption (see here and here). This is demonstrated in the widening gap between IP and retail sales (Figure 3).

Source: NBS, J.P. Morgan
Figure 3: Uneven recovery in demand and supply in China Seasonally adjusted, Dec 2019=100
![](images/60abe618914776d8d68e4aeedb31fd445a14a525c585beb4af97c6f3b9a3abf6.jpg)

## It is pervasive

It is not just deflation in the aggregate but its pervasiveness that is disconcerting. Across almost all measures of inflation (both consumer and producer), the pace of price rises since the reopening has been markedly lower than their the prepandemic averages (Figure 4).

Figure 4: China prices
![](images/35c8e89b42abf3ef488506429ce671b3d9cf826d0a5c426b0d87b3fcdd456e48.jpg)

This is also true for asset prices. Stock prices have fallen 18.5% since late 2021, while home prices have declined 13% since Aug 21 (link).

China’s deflation, however, has helped global goods disinflation (link). Along with the weakening in CNY, China’s USD export prices have declined nearly 16% since January 2023 and are still falling (Figure 5). Our estimates suggest a 10%-pt fall in China’s export price lowers global (ex China) goods inflation 0.7%-pts, two quarters after the shock.

Figure 5: China's export price (USD)
![](images/703c5d014b1795f103b2379575a4ce0a13f8385afd305bc6935ad908f8228f80.jpg)
The pervasive deflation in China has lowered nominal growth across the economy. Barring transport and restaurants, all other sectors over the past 6 quarters recorded lower nominal growth than before the pandemic (Figure 6).

Figure 6: China sectoral nominal growth %oya, average quarterly growth rate
![](images/112fdf1408c23ea33b88bdb3a65c58f78c404935708eb439ad131ef750723a5b.jpg)

While housing has been worst hit, downward price pressures have been intense in agriculture, mining, manufacturing, wholesale and retail trade, construction and financial services (Figure 7).

Figure 7: Sectors under price pressure
![](images/e166b2c2543d55ce56fdcb60eac9871ad30c7bbab5b05fea1bbdbd37e11558f0.jpg)

## And pernicious

Downward price pressure implies weak pricing power for corporates, hence profit margins and profitability have also been hit in a low nominal GDP growth environment (Figure 8).

Figure 9: China's total social debt

Figure 8: Nominal GDP growth vs. profit growth
![](images/6982b8aea89b1e9d8f0161b6d37264b383948b1ef70513ac69acdb8d5892a434.jpg)
Source: NBS, J.P. Morgan

Deflation/disinflation, in principle, can improve household purchasing power. But this has not been reflected in China’s consumption growth because of weak consumer confidence reflecting falling asset prices, low job and wage growth, and dampened expectations of near-term economic recovery. With housing making up 60% of total household assets , the ongoing housing price correction has had significant negative wealth effect on China’s consumption. Our earlier study (link) suggests that a 1% fall in house prices lowers consumption growth by 0.22%. Secondary housing prices have dropped 13% since their peak in mid-2021.

## Fiscal revenue gets hit

Low nominal GDP growth has also dragged down growth in tax revenues. This has limited how much government spending can be raised to support growth, adding to China’s already high fiscal deficit and pushing up debt too rapidly. In 2023, the implicit nominal GDP growth on which revenue projections were based was 7.4%. Nominal GDP growth turned out to be only 4.6%. This shortfall in nominal GDP growth prompted a contraction in receipts from consumption taxes (-3.5%), individual income taxes (-1%), and property-related taxes (-12.5%) in 2023. In addition, land sale revenue (the largest off-budgetary funding source for local governments) fell 23% in 2022 and 13% in 2023, and could decline another 15% this year.

## Debt dynamics worsen

Beyond the pressures on the budget, China’s debt dynamics have also been worsened by deflation. While China’s augmented fiscal deficit (including not only budgetary items but also off-budgetary items such as net land sales and LGFV borrowing) has averaged at 10.4% of GDP since the GFC (2009-2023), debt funding had been kept manageable by land sales and a very favorable growth-interest rate differential. While the fall in land sales has increased debt funding, the collapse in the growth-interest rate differential has also been a major factor in the deterioration of debt metrics.

China’s debt level has increased rapidly since 2008 (Figure 9). Based on our estimates, total non-financial debt in the economy rose from 143% of GDP) in 2008 to an estimated 305% of GDP in 2024. The rise in central government debt has been modest (from 19% to 26% of GDP), but the other components have all observed dramatic increases. These include local government debt (from zero to 34% of GDP), household debt (from 18% to 62% of GDP) and corporate debt (from 105% to 180% of GDP, including LGFV debt.

![](images/d40cf03fc25c69bfabe2e034df7d1fc8e6736b94193ef41d9aa547668e50ad33.jpg)

The pace of debt build-up has accelerated since 2022 to an annual rate of 10% of GDP even as credit growth decelerated from 13.3% oya in 2020 to 8.1%oya in June 2024. This gap between the rise in the stock of debt and slower credit flow is because of the sharp narrowing of China’s growth-interest differential (Figure 10). From 2002-11, this differential averaged 13.5-pts, keeping debt dynamics manageable despite the large investment-led and credit-fueled expansion. The differential dropped to 6%-pts over 2012-19, raising the debt burden. It has now collapsed to just over 2%-pts since 2023 making it far more difficult to stabilize debt without reducing credit growth more sharply, which China cannot afford to do in the face of weak growth, in our view.

Figure 10: China's growth-interest rate differential %-pts, nominal GDP growth less 5Y CGB yield
![](images/2013f61e02235991047f178eaf42adcd1f7ba338bfcfffda5a906348d0c932c5.jpg)

## China needs policy redirection, not more of the same

Naturally, we think policy needs to be redirected from just focusing on supporting the real economy to also ending deflationary pressures. As we have long argued, while policy stimulus is still needed, changing the direction of policy is more important now (link). Since the reopening, authorities have focused exclusively on supply-side support (production and investment) to boost growth. While real GDP growth has been managed at close to the 5% target with these measures, it has come at the cost of a widening gap between production and consumption (Figure 3). This has likely piled up inventories, which has spilled into export price deflation as China has struggled to find a home market for its output. Thus, we think a directional shift in policy towards supporting consumption, rather than more stimulus of the same kind, is needed to reduce the excess capacity, end deflation, boost the weak pricing power of firms to get earnings and profit growth up, support tax revenues, and help improve debt dynamics.

The recently concluded meeting of the politburo of the CPC has prioritized support for social welfare, consumption, and household incomes. We await follow-up measures from the government. In the absence of a meaningful redirection of policies, while we think the 2024 real GDP growth target of 5% will likely be missed modestly at 4.7%, the miss in the implicit 7.4% nominal GDP growth forecast (based on budget projections) could be more significant at a likely 4.1% (link).

Figure 1: ASEAN US\$ liquidity buffer

## ASEAN external liquidity buffers: ample but divergent

• ASEAN’s US\$ liquidity is broadly ample based on IMF’s ARA metric but with wide in-region variation

• Reserve adequacy levels differ by economic/BOP structures

● Dollarization behavior on negative/narrow rate differentials puts more pressure on reserves

• Policy responses also vary according to US\$ liquidity constraints

Using the IMF’s comprehensive ARA methodology, we find that US\$ liquidity is broadly ample in ASEAN, but with wide variation between countries. Most of the decrease in liquidity buffers since 2021 is the result of reserves drawdown instead of worsening fundamentals. Reserve adequacy (RA) levels differ by economic/BOP structures. and additional caveats apply when it comes to the role of short-term external debt. Going forward, if negative/narrow rate differentials with the US persist, the region’s reserves could face added pressure from dollarization behavior. This has and will likely continue to generate differential policy responses across countries as they seek to manage currency volatility.

## Casting a wider net on reserve assessment

When it comes to assessing the adequacy of a central bank’s international reserves, investors tend to adopt heuristics in the form of coverage ratios (e.g., imports, short-term external debt). While easily computed and understood, this approach does not fully capture the different external risks and vulnerabilities of an economy, making cross-country comparisons less effective. For example, a country with a superior import coverage ratio does not necessarily have more adequate reserves if it is more susceptible to other types of FX outflows (e.g., portfolio, debt repayment).

To better facilitate “apples-to-apples” comparison, we apply the IMF’s Assessing Reserve Adequacy (ARA) methodology. This calculates a country’s required level of reserves (RA) to address external shocks, based on the strength of inflows (i.e., exports) and the vulnerability to outflows (e.g., short-term external debt, foreign portfolio liability stock, and domestic money supply). The ratio between domestic US\$ liquidity (sum of central bank net reserves and excess US\$ liquidity in the domestic banking system) and IMF’s RA is then interpreted as a country’s reserves buffer.

## Wide variation in US\$ liquidity buffers

Based on IMF guidance, a reserves/ARA metric ratio of 1.0-1.5x is considered adequate. Using this standard, our analysis shows that reserves across ASEAN are adequate as at 4Q23, albeit with the caveat of a broader measure of reserves (i.e., include US\$ liquidity in domestic banks).

However, there is a fair degree of variation within the region (Table 1), where the Philippines (3.7x) and Thailand (2.6x) enjoy significantly higher US\$ liquidity buffers than Indonesia (1.4x) and Malaysia (1.1x). Even on a historical basis, US\$ liquidity buffers in the Philippines and Thailand have improved more than Indonesia and Malaysia in recent years (Figure 1).
