The bulk of changes in US\$ liquidity buffers since the start of the post-pandemic tightening in external conditions (from Fed rate hikes and a commodity price shock) is in turn, attributed to changes in US\$ liquidity itself (Figure 2), where central bank reserves were used to manage volatility. This also means that ASEAN’s macro fundamentals or external risk profile have not materially changed, as evident in a stable reserve adequacy (RA) requirement (Figure 3).

## Identifying the stress points

Given the variation in economic/BOP structures, it is not surprising that RA levels show compositional differences (Table 2) across ASEAN.

For example, exports take up a higher share of RA for tradedependent economies (e.g., Malaysia, Thailand; Figure 4), while more reserves, percentage wise, are needed to cover a pullback in foreign portfolio debt flows for twin-deficit economies (e.g., Indonesia, Philippines).

The Philippines have the lowest RA in the region but if adjusted by the size of the economy (8% of GDP), it is roughly on par with Indonesia’s (6% of GDP). Still, the Philippines’ RA compares favorably with Thailand’s (18% of GDP) and Malaysia’s (23% of GDP), likely because of differences in debt levels and financialization.

Figure 4: IMF Assessing Reserve Adequacy (ARA) metric
![](images/e38774f87bb403d8f231bc2d02bf41172b70ffded5702cb85ac1a73fa512ac41.jpg)
Source: IMF, J.P. Morgan estimates

Another key observation is the relatively high RA level for Malaysia, which can be attributed to short-term external debt (8% of GDP, 36% of RA). But the caveat is that the banking sector constitutes an outsized portion of short-term external debt (Figure 5), specifically in intragroup borrowings, reflecting the regional footprint of Malaysian banks. This may be a mitigating factor, depending on the liquidity, capital adequacy, and risk profile (e.g., FX, duration mismatch) of the Malaysian banking sector.

Figure 5: Short-term external debt
![](images/e7fa1ac02f40333cdf2391ef67fcdff039fb7e2f8b4da462f2757f5d65ac0d93.jpg)

## Watch out for dollarization

Intra-regional variations and country idiosyncrasies aside, ASEAN is facing a common issue in the dollarization of savings, as seen in the rise of foreign currency deposits (Figure 6) amid record-low interest rate differentials with the US

(Figure 7). The active conversion from local currency to foreign currency deposits, due to yield searching behavior, if it persists, exerts more pressure on central bank reserves, especially on economies with high M2/GDP ratios (e.g., Thailand and Malaysia).

Figure 6: Foreign currency deposit % share of total deposit
![](images/536ece533fd7069016e3f9278d775a9532742b5abb894442b0c92ce1a4e41513.jpg)

Figure 7: ASEAN-US policy rate differential
% p.a., ASEAN less US, period average

Source: CEIC, J.P. Morgan forecast for 2023/24

## Different buffers mean different responses

Variation in US\$ liquidity buffers amid persistent dollarization pressures ha induced differences in central bank responses to currency pressures. Countries with stronger buffers (e.g., Thailand, Philippines) are generally more comfortable tapping into spot reserves to manage currency volatility, while countries with weaker buffers resort to moral suasion and administrative measures. In the case of Malaysia, state-owned investment funds have been asked to repatriate foreign investment income. In the case of Vietnam, onshore US\$ deposit rates have been capped at zero to curb dollarization behavior.

However, by and large, monetary policy is still driven by traditional reaction functions, regardless of US\$ liquidity buffers. Despite strong buffers, BSP has a track record of “following the Fed” in policy tightening due to the high FX passthrough to CPI. In contrast, despite weaker reserve buffers, BNM’s policy rate regime is more stable through the ebbs and flows of FX performance as its reaction function is more sensitive to domestic conditions, specifically core inflation.

## United States

The July jobs report was a sizable disappointment, as the unemployment rate jumped 0.2%-pt to 4.3%...

• … and nonfarm payrolls rose a soft 114k, with downward revisions to prior months

We now see the Fed cutting 50bp at the next two meetings, then 25bp thereafter to 3% next year

Other labor market data this week show cooling wage growth but elevated jobless claims

What a difference two days make. The July jobs report was a big downside surprise with fairly broad-based signs of weakening, including much-softer-than-expected job growth (up 114,000) and jump in the unemployment rate to 4.3%. Had the Fed had this report in hand going into the FOMC meeting this past Wednesday, it almost certainly would have cut the policy rate by at least 25bp. Now that it looks to be materially behind the curve, we expect a 50bp cut at the September meeting, followed by another 50bp cut in November. From a risk-management perspective, even somewhat stronger inflation or labor market data in the intervening releases should not preclude the Fed from catching up more quickly.

Indeed, a case could be made for an intermeeting easing, especially if the data soften further—although Fed officials might worry about how such a move could be (mis)interpreted. Regardless, with Chair Powell stressing in his press conference that the Fed is carefully watching for signs the labor market could be more than just cooling, attention will be keenly focused on his remarks at the Jackson Hole Symposium later this month, if not sooner. Dovish Chicago Fed president Goolsbee, who was the first Fed official to address the July jobs report, surprisingly advocated for caution and to not overreact to one month’s number—but also declared that the Fed needed to balance policy with the economic data in “short order”and that multiple rate cuts are in the cards.

Looking beyond the next two FOMC meetings, we see the Fed continuing to cut 25bp per meeting until it gets the policy rate much closer to neutral. Assuming that is around 3%, the easing cycle would extend into 3Q25. Overall, this is a more accelerated easing pace than we had previously expected or the Fed had signaled in the now-stale June dot plot.

## Looking Sahm-what worse

The details of the July jobs report were not good. The disappointing payroll gains reflected weaker growth across several sectors, and prior months were revised 29k lower. We had flagged the possibility that the impact of Hurricane Beryl could bias down both payrolls and the workweek, while biasing up average hourly earnings. In the event, the workweek was modestly softer (ticking down to 34.2) but wage growth rose just 0.2% overall and 0.3% for production and non-supervisory workers, easing the year-ago growth rates to 3.6% and 3.8%, respectively. The BLS concluded no measurable effect on the data from Beryl, and the distortions in the establishment survey may not have been large despite big jumps in people reporting they did not work or worked fewer than usual hours in the household survey.

The household survey should have been less impacted by Beryl by construction, leaving the two-tenths rise in the unemployment rate to 4.3% that much more concerning. This triggered the Sahm Rule as typically measured (Figure 1), but more importantly suggested a shift to a greater contribution from inflows into unemployment; the previous drift higher in the u-rate largely reflected a slowdown in outflows. Both household employment and unemployment rose with a gain in labor force as the participation rate rose another tenth to 62.7%. More encouragingly, prime-aged participation jumped 0.3%-pt to 84.0%— its highest rate since 2001. But that largely was the extent of any good news in the July release.

Figure 1: Real-time Sahm Rule
![](images/766c7cf96317ecd0faa9a7550a49bae79d2e2f349e3d9440011edcd2133fb157.jpg)

## In other labor market news….

Prior to the July jobs report, other labor market data this week were mostly pointing to some further gradual cooling, as Powell cited in his remarks. The June JOLTS report showed openings, hires, quits and layoffs all declining relative to May. The vacancy/unemployment ratio edged down to 1.20, a new cycle low. The job openings rate was stable at 4.9%, but this was boosted by a second consecutive month of surging openings in government (Figure 2).

On the wage front the news was generally favorable and should reinforce Powell’s belief that the labor market is not contributing to inflationary risks. Labor cost pressures eased in 2Q relative to the beginning of the year, when we saw some unexpected strength in both the employment cost index (ECI) and unit labor costs (ULC). The latter can be quite noisy, but the former is the Fed’s preferred gauge of wage inflation and eased to 3.7%q/q, saar after having popped 4.8% in 1Q. Much like the JOLTS data, the ECI revealed still fairly

Source: BLS, J.P. Morgan

elevated public sector compensation growth (4.9% for state and local employees versus 3.5% for private workers).

Figure 2: Job openings rate %, sa, both axes
![](images/3b2bbc60b6b0dd56e7437d6c7a8c997d9346028fcf49cb8fdae059572b2b1c58.jpg)

More timely news came with the weekly jobless claims report, which gave a more nuanced picture as both initial and continuing claims moved up, albeit with some residual noise from Hurricane Beryl arguably still affecting the levels. Initial jobless claims for the week ending July 27 jumped to a new high for this year of 249k (from 235k the prior week), and the four-week average approached its recent high from late June. Continuing claims, which shown some stability in recent weeks, broke higher again to 1.877mm. Questions about seasonality have dogged the interpretation of claims lately, and over the past few weeks the NSA initial claims numbers appear to be broadly following the pattern of prior years lower. But the recent rise in continuing claims remains in the NSA data, echoing the more concerning message of labor market weakness in the July employment report.

## Manufacturing surveys: divergent declines

The manufacturing PMI and ISM surveys both weakened in July and point to softness in that sector. But the ISM survey is conveying a considerably more negative message, which so far has not been backed up in the activity data. The ISM composite declined to 46.8, which is barely above the current cycle low (Figure 3). By contrast, the trend in the manufacturing PMI has been modestly higher, with July marking the first decline in three months. While the latest reading of 49.6 is off its earlier peak and at the lower end of what is typically seen during economic expansions, the PMI remains comfortably above its cycle low. The modest uptrend in the PMI also appears more consistent with the actual activity data: manufacturing IP rose 3.6% on a 3m/3m annualized basis in June.

For the ISM survey, the most concerning part was the fall in the employment index, which plunged 6pts to 43.4. That was the lowest level of the current cycle and may point to job losses ahead, whereas the manufacturing PMI employment index does not signal weakness. In the event, the July employment report showed no change in manufacturing jobs.

Manufacturing survey composite indexes
![](images/d56de82856c96a247f16a5edf807ebc75fa1d9871cd07ce4be7041f2da1e6d99.jpg)
Source: ISM, S&P Global, J.P. Morgan

## Home sales and construction data softened

Pending home sales, which lead existing home sales by one to two months, increased 5%m/m in June — albeit from an alltime low. When combined with still-soft weekly mortgage purchase applications, the data are consistent with existing home sales largely moving sideways at very low levels in the near term. The more timely news now for the housing market is what will happen to mortgage rates. With interest rate expectations dropping after the jobs report, mortgage rates could be set for a step down that would likely benefit sales later this year provided a weaker labor market doesn’t materially undermine demand for housing. Construction spending for residential and nonresidential buildings also fell in June, and the trajectory points to the possibility for another drop in private structures investment in 3Q. The decline in housing starts to date should lead to further residential spending reductions in the months ahead, unless improvements begin rising again. For nonresidential building, the surge in manufacturing construction from new technology facilities is continuing but at a slower rate, while spending on most other building types is starting to fall (Figure 4).

Figure 4: Private manufacturing construction: total and ex-computers \$bn, saar
![](images/8b369c03ef6ad482919fcc2ca5a359f0fb3636ca35c2352fa745eb9407c2f270.jpg)

Next week will be very light on data. The most important release will be the ISM services survey, with attention focused on whether a large plunge in new orders in June is at least partially reversed. We will also get the latest Senior Loan Officer Opinion Survey , which will offer a look at how credit conditions among banks were changing during 2Q.

We forecast that the final July reading on the services PMI will be unchanged from the flash value of 56.0. As discussed in the forecast for the ISM non-manufacturing survey, the services PMI has looked surprisingly strong relative to other surveys recently, and it would not be surprising if it weakened in coming months. It is possible such a drop could happen in the final July survey, though usually the flash and final values are quite close. Based on past patterns if there was a decline in the final July report it would likely be no more than one point.

We look for the ISM non-manufacturing index to rise slightly from 48.8 in June to 50.0 in July. The index has been steadily softening for close to a year, but it has also been very volatile in recent months, and the value in June looked surprisingly low, especially with new orders collapsing dramatically to levels usually associated with recession. Consequently we think there is room for it to recover a bit, though our forecast would still be lower than the trailing three-month average of 50.7.

Other business surveys sent mixed signals during the month. The services PMI actually improved in July and is unusually high relative to the ISM non-manufacturing survey, which could argue for more of a recovery in the ISM survey. On the other hand the trend of these two surveys has been significantly different in recent months, and the average business activity reading from regional Fed services surveys worsened for a second consecutive month. The Fed surveys have been running unusually low relative to the ISM ever since COVID, which makes it harder to know what they imply for the level of the ISM, but their direction of travel is weaker, as their recent drop reverses an improvement over the first half of the year.

We expect the nominal goods and services trade balance, which was -\$75.1bn in May, to narrow to between -\$73bn and -\$72bn in June. Advance goods trade on a Census basis, which is close to the BOP basis in this report, was already released for June, and it showed the goods deficit shrinking during the month on the back of strong growth in exports. For services, we expect to see ongoing growth in exports and imports that should lead the surplus to continue to slowly increase. The services balance has been rebounding from its post-COVID low as segments like net exports of travel recover.

Payroll survey week</td><td></td></tr><tr><td></td><td></td></tr></table>

We project that initial jobless claims will pull back to around 240k in the week ending August 3 from 249k the prior week. The four-week average would rise to 242k from 238k, which would be just slightly above the prior high of 239k for this year, which was set at the end of June. Jobless claims last week had popped up by 14k. While a gradual loosening in the labor market could be causing claims to drift up, the lingering effects of Hurricane Beryl still appeared to be propping up claims in Texas last week by around 10k, so claims could drop in the latest week assuming Texas reverts to normal patterns.

Jobless claims rose last month during the summer before declining by the fall, and because claims rose at a similar time this year there are lingering questions around the residual seasonality in this series. We discussed this at more length here, noting that NSA claims were in fact more elevated than usual last year during the summer, but that this was boosted by unusual changes in Ohio, Texas, and Minnesota. When these states are excluded claims were much more similar to earlier years, and claims for this year are tracking quite close to prior year patterns. If the labor market loosens further then claims should increase over time. On the other hand if claims continue to follow the pattern from last year then seasonally adjusted claims would move lower by the winter. Last year initial claims were unusually low in the winter, likely stemming from lower agriculture and construction claims, and that pattern was repeated again during January and February of this year. Assuming that winter temperatures do not turn sharply colder than the last couple years the lower pattern of claims could repeat.

Continuing jobless claims have also increased recently, though they have shown some signs of stability over the last four weeks. While they rose again in the week ending July 20, claims stemming from Hurricane Beryl were likely a contributing factor. Excluding Texas, continuing claims rose slightly but were still similar to the prior month.

## Consumer confidence (Jul 30)

JOLTS
<table><tr><td></td><td>May</td><td>Jun</td><td>Jul</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Conference Bd index</td><td>101.3</td><td></td><td>100.4 97.8</td><td>100.0 100.3</td></tr><tr><td>Present situation</td><td>140.8</td><td>141.5 135.3</td><td></td><td>133.6</td></tr><tr><td>Jobs plentiful</td><td>37.0</td><td>-38.1 35.5</td><td></td><td>34.1</td></tr><tr><td>Jobs hard to get</td><td>14.3</td><td>-14.1 15.7</td><td></td><td>16.0</td></tr><tr><td>Labor mkt diff</td><td>22.7</td><td>24.0 19.8</td><td></td><td>18.1</td></tr><tr><td>Expectations</td><td>74.9</td><td>73.0 72.8</td><td></td><td>78.2</td></tr></table>

US consumer confidence rose to 100.3 in July from 97.8 in June (originally 100.4) as confidence continues to remain quite subdued. Probably the most interesting takeaway was a soft signal on the job market, as the labor market differential (jobs plentiful less jobs hard to get) fell to 18.1% in July from a downward revised 19.8% (originally 24.0%) in June. This differential is negatively correlated with the unemployment rate, and it is still steadily tracking lower, which makes sense in light of the July increase in the unemployment rate.

Job openings declined in the June JOLTS report to 8.184mm , and while this came on top of an upward revision to May the trend is down. The labor market continues to come into better balance, with the openings to unemployed ratio declining to 1.20 in June from 1.24 in July, with both readings being similar to pre-COVID. The layoff rate slipped to 0.9% from 1.1%, a low reading (there is no sign of layoffs increasing in this report), while quits fell by over 100k, consistent with workers likely seeing fewer openings that can spur job-to-job transitions.

## Employment cost index (Jul 31)

<table><tr><td></td><td>4Q23</td><td>1Q24</td><td>2Q24</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Total(%q/q, sa)</td><td>0.9</td><td>1.2</td><td>11</td><td>0.9</td></tr><tr><td>Wages and salaries</td><td>1.1</td><td>1.1</td><td>1</td><td>0.9</td></tr><tr><td>Benefits</td><td>0.7</td><td>1.1</td><td>09</td><td>1.0</td></tr><tr><td>Total(%oya,nsa)</td><td>4.2</td><td>4.2</td><td>4.2</td><td>4.1</td></tr><tr><td>Wages and salaries</td><td>4.3</td><td>4.4</td><td>43</td><td>4.2</td></tr><tr><td>Benefits</td><td>3.8</td><td>3.7</td><td>37</td><td>3.8</td></tr></table>

The ECI rose 0.9%q/q in 2Q (3.7%q/q annualized) and the year-ago rate edged down to 4.1% from 4.2% the prior quarter. Compensation growth is slowly moderating, with the year-over-year rate the lowest since the end of 2021, though over the last year the rate has come down only 0.4%-pt. Given recent signs that the labor market has become softer, and with price inflation falling back toward the Fed’s target 2% target, we would expect that wage growth will continue to moderate in coming quarters.

Pending home sales popped 4.8%m/m in June after having hit their all-time low in May. The latest increase only does a little to reverse recent weakness and pending sales, which lead existing home sales by 1-2 months, are still lower than three months ago. The overall message here, consistent with what we’ve seen from weekly mortgage purchase applications, is that home sales are broadly moving sideways at a depressed level. Mortgage applications are down between June and July, though they hadn’t been running quite as weak as pending sales to begin with.

Productivity rebounded in 2Q to 2.3%q/q saar after a weak 0.4% increase in 1Q while unit labor cost growth was 0.9%q q saar and was up just 0.5%oya, a soft reading. Productivity has now risen 2.7%oya, a solid result in the context of recent years.

Construction spending (Aug 1)
S&P Global manufacturing PMI (US final)

There was not much new information in the final July manufacturing PMI, as the composite was hardly changed vs the flash release (rose to 49.6 from 49.5). That value is up slightly from a year earlier but is still on the low end during economic expansions. The output index improved 1 point to 50.5 vs. the flash, while the employment index fell back 0.7 point to 51.6. Employment is down from the recent high of 53.5 in June but this comes on the back of a string of increases through the first half of the year and employment is still well above soft readings late last year.

ISM manufacturing survey (Aug 1)
<table><tr><td>sa</td><td>May</td><td>Jun</td><td>Jul</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Overall index</td><td>48.7</td><td>48.5</td><td>49.0</td><td>46.8</td></tr><tr><td>Production</td><td>50.2</td><td>48.5</td><td></td><td>45.9</td></tr><tr><td>New orders</td><td>45.4</td><td>49.3</td><td></td><td>47.4</td></tr><tr><td>Inventories</td><td>47.9</td><td>45.4</td><td></td><td>44.5</td></tr><tr><td>Employment</td><td>51.1</td><td>49.3</td><td></td><td>43.4</td></tr><tr><td>Supplier deliveries</td><td>48.9</td><td>49.8</td><td></td><td>52.6</td></tr><tr><td>Export orders</td><td>50.6</td><td>48.8</td><td></td><td>49.0</td></tr><tr><td>Imports</td><td>51.1</td><td>48.5</td><td></td><td>48.6</td></tr><tr><td>Prices</td><td>57.0</td><td>52.1</td><td></td><td>52.9</td></tr></table>

The headline July ISM manufacturing index disappointed, which unexpectedly slid to 46.8, down 1.7-pts from 48.5 in June, to a level last seen in late 2023. While not weak enough to signal a meaningful risk of a downturn (ISM suggests that would require a reading below 43), it does signal some further loss of momentum in the manufacturing sector even as actual activity data have been holding up better than the ISM would suggest. In the details, employment saw the largest monthly decline, down 5.9-pts to 43.4, its lowest reading since June 2020, at the start of the pandemic recovery. Production was down 2.6-pts to a cycle low as well (45.9). ISM new orders declined 1.9-pts in July to 47.4, near its average over the past year. The prices also picked up to 52.9 (+0.8-pts); the trend has been modestly upward since late 2022 but is measurably below where it was in the spring.

Construction spending fell 0.3%m/m in June, the second consecutive decline, and taking into account revisions to May and April this report shouldn’t yield much in the way of revisions to 2Q GDP. Total private residential construction fell 0.3%m/m as single-family spending declines accelerated though multifamily and improvements were up. Multifamily should turn down again soon given the past decline in starts and residential investment in the GDP accounts will likely keep falling this quarter. Nonresidential spending was down marginally (0.1%) as manufacturing, which has been a standout segment, recorded hardly any gain on the month. Public construction fell 0.4%m/m; public spending growth had been strong but growth has slowed sharply since last fall.

The July jobs report came in surprisingly weak, as the unemployment rate rose to 4.3% from 4.1% in June. The U6 underemployment rate jumped 0.4%-pts to 7.8%. Non-farm payrolls rose just 114K in July, with downward revisions of 29K in prior months. A few services sectors reported declines in payrolls on the month, and government employment was softer than its recent run rate at 17K. Hours worked ticked down to 34.2 as expected, even as the BLS suggested Hurricane Beryl had no measurable impact on response rates or the data. Average hourly earnings rose 0.2% on the month, and 3.6% over year ago levels, in part reflecting downward revisions to prior months. This report certainly should move concern about labor market weakness front and center for the Fed.

Source: BLS, J.P. Morgan

## Focus: Job finding and rising unemployment

This week’s data pointed to a weakening in the labor market, though also highlighted areas of strength that have nonetheless failed to arrest the upward drift in the unemployment rate. In particular, layoff rates in the June JOLTS fell and have broadly been edging down since early 2023 (Figure 1). The message from this is that weaker hiring without a layoff increase was sufficient to drive higher unemployment. Compounding this challenge, the July employment report’s 0.2%-pt increase in the unemployment rate came despite a stable job finding rate that month, indicating layoffs may also start to add upward pressure to unemployment.

Figure 1: Monthly layoff rates (through June)
![](images/fd0da83b204f2b4bd22d1fda1b1cde8bd63ebb136cd7e74d27d84dd022a39196.jpg)

One can also construct a measure of layoffs using the household survey (CPS) by observing workers who made a transition out of employment in a month due to a layoff. In a recent study, Ellieroth and Michaud (2024) construct such a layoff measure (Figure 1). The monthly CPS survey only registers a layoff when a worker (a) exits employment and (b) does not take another job by the time they are interviewed in the next month. Consequently, this CPS layoff rate is sensitive to the chance of finding new work during the month, as opposed to the JOLTS measure which captures all layoffs by surveying employers. Regardless of whether changes in this CPS rate are being driven by rising layoffs or falling hiring, the rate has notably increased in recent months. Its level, which is available through June, is not that much higher than 2019, but the upward move is potentially concerning if it signals more than a normalization following a period at historically low levels. Similar increases in the layoff rate have often, though not always, preceded recessions.

The re-employment rate, the fraction of the unemployed who transition to employment in a given month, is also useful for understanding hiring. Because unemployed workers can also enter employment by moving out of the labor force and later re-entering it, we complement the re-employment probability with the job finding rate introduced by Robert Shimer, which accounts for this. Both measures had been strong after COVID but have since declined a similar amount, signalling that unemployed workers are increasingly struggling to find employment, with the probabilities now slightly worse than in 2019 (Figure 2). However, the monthly job finding rate was stable in July even as unemployment jumped.

Figure 2: Measures of unemployment outflow
![](images/c2ff240167d12ee7fc66f34cc50e82d1aadfbb09e7e6b82cd90fe7c5299386f3.jpg)

Returning to the hiring rate reported in JOLTS, declines now put it back at levels last seen in 2014. But because quits have simultaneously fallen, employment growth has only slowed a bit, unlike the sharp deceleration one would expect if the economy were tipping into recession. Indeed it’s not clear whether quits are so low because hiring is low, or vice versa. For instance, very high levels of job re-allocation immediately following the pandemic might have pulled forward planned job switches, which could later result in low quits.

Figure 3: Hiring and separation rates vs hires/openings ratio
![](images/14d1b62350dce6506152f048a5744b2cf7fe80450c6b4bbe79b7eeffc3b6eebd.jpg)

A way to disentangle this is to compare hires to job openings (Figure 3), which will be low when the labor market is strong and hires cannot be made because of a lack of available workers. In 2Q this ratio was 16% below the 2019 level and 34% below the 2014 level, supporting the argument that the labor market is stronger than the hiring rate alone would suggest, although the series may have a downward trend making comparisons across time more challenging. That being said, it has been gently rising from all-time lows over the last couple years, indicative of gradually normalizing conditions, and as noted previously hiring for unemployed workers has not been strong enough to stop the rise in the unemployment rate.

## Euro Area

• Euro area growth outlook has become more uncertain

• Sharply lower manufacturing PMI, a (modest) labor market softening and German underperformance

• A noisy upside surprise in July inflation, but hints of softer momentum still in place

• Euro area unemployment nudged up in June

This week’s data further reinforced the case for ECB rate cuts, which we expect in September and December. Following on from last week’s disappointing flash PMI, this week’s flash 2Q24 GDP report was weaker than we had expected. GDP rose 1%q/q saar, broadly in line with 1Q24, with persistent stagnation in Germany the main source of disappointment. While there are still some questions about the linkage between the PMI and GDP, their signals aligned in 1H24 overall, which means that the weaker PMI in July is sending a message of growth downshifting further to below 1%ar (Figure 1). We continue to think that a further downshift is unlikely as the broader fundamentals in the region are not all negative, but growth risks are on the downside until upcoming PMI reports give some reassurance.

Figure 1: Tracking Euro area GDP growth with the PMI
%q/q saar, model with dummies for 4Q08, 1Q09 and 3Q10-4Q12
![](images/130066257e3ee694a605a6f6a2e4d9c8ca057ebedd3d536230bda7ce22312470.jpg)

On the inflation side, this week’s flash HICP report for July surprised on the upside. Core inflation held at 2.9%oya, even though we had expected a deceleration to 2.7%oya. However, the details showed a 0.31%m/m, sa, gain in services prices, which was in line with June and well below the 0.45%m/m, sa, average seen in the first five months of the year. And, the 0.30%m/m, sa, jump in core goods prices was very narrowly based in Italy, where seasonal sales may have generated a temporary effect. Overall, we think the report shows the broader disinflation process still on track, even if at a slow pace. And, for the ECB, weaker growth also matters as it raises the likelihood of further progress in the disinflation journey. As a result, we see further cuts in September and December as very likely, and can now also see a third cut in October if the inflation data show clearer progress and the PMI declines further.

## Germany weighs on Euro area GDP

We went into this week’s 2Q24 flash GDP prints with a forecast of 1.5%q/q saar, which was around double the consensus expectation. Most countries printed very close to our expectation, with Spain standing out with a strong gain of 3.3%q/q saar. However, a large disappointment in Germany, where GDP contracted 0.3%q/q saar (versus our forecast of a 1.25%q/q saar increase), pulled down the Euro area aggregate to 1%q/q saar. Excluding Germany and the volatile Irish data, GDP grew by a decent 1.3%q/q saar in the region.

Figure 2: Euro area GDP
![](images/92e8eee2f076802b8c897611dd332517f256304078dc2e23ac4abf55fde1d88a.jpg)

There are very few details to assess this further. The German statistics office pointed to a decline in construction, which likely reflected weather-related payback after a strong 1Q24, and to a decline in spending on machinery and equipment, which sends a more negative signal about German corporates. But, even on this, we need to wait for the details of the final report as government investment can also affect this, for example through lumpy spending on military equipment. That the statistics office did not mention consumer spending suggests that it did not make much of a contribution to 2Q24 GDP, which would be disappointing in itself given the likely strength in real incomes.

Elsewhere, the GDP reports were better overall. But, the GDP increases in France (1.1%q/q saar) and Spain (3.3%q/q saar) saw details that were relatively unexciting in terms of domestic demand. Instead, net trade contributed to the outcomes in both countries, reflecting stronger exports and weak imports. The former is encouraging, but the latter also makes the domestic details somewhat underwhelming.

Another area that was mixed in the GDP reports was the news on the GDP deflator. It increased by only 1.5%q/q saar in France and by 0.2%q/q saar in Spain, which left the annual rates at just 2.1%oya in France and at 3.2%oya in Spain. But, there was a larger increase of 5.9%q/q saar in Germany, which left its annual rate at 4.0%oya. We suspect that these data will be modestly encouraging for the ECB as they may hint at unit profits continuing to make a more modest contribution to inflationary pressures. But, a stronger signal on this was prevented by the German report. It is interesting that the GDP deflator is running strongest in Germany, despite its underperformance on output.

## Euro area unemployment nudges up

The Euro area unemployment rate unexpectedly increased 0.1%-pt to 6.5% in June. The number of unemployed increased by 41,000, having increased by 34,000 in May. Before that, the number of unemployed was broadly stable (or down) and therefore it is a bit surprising that increases over the last months have already sufficed to push the unemployment rate up by a tenth. However, a broadly stable trend in the unemployment rate is consistent with an economy that is struggling to generate above-trend growth. In this context, there are no real signs of outright job losses, which is unsurprising given that the PMI employment index and actual employment data still point to gains. Hence, the softer unemployment data over the past two months likely reflect the pace of hiring not quite keeping up with the growth of the labour force. Directionally, a move up in the unemployment rate helps in terms of the disinflation process, but, of course, the quantitative effect of a 0.1%-pt increase on wages and inflation is small on its own. And, there is likely still significant support for the labour market from elevated levels of labour shortages that companies are reporting in the EC survey and also from softness in the workweek.

Figure 3: Euro area unemployment
![](images/ff2c7334c677ff855dc26a61f69a4a5da5314acb2ba6afda82b32de2b07574f3.jpg)

## A noisy upside surprise on inflation

The modest and uncertain news on growth and the modest tick higher in unemployment make the expected disinflation a bit more likely. In this context, the incoming news on inflation is also encouraging, but in a very noisy way. Core prices rose 0.30%m/m, sa, faster than the 0.23%m/m, sa, seen in June and in line with the average seen in the first five months of the year (Figure 4). However, two factors played a role. First, a sudden jump in core goods prices of 0.30%m/m, sa, was very narrowly based in Italy, where clothing/footwear inflation jumped due to seasonal sales. These were either less intensive than last year or the timing was different. We sus-

pect that this effect will unwind in August. Second, a calculation effect may have impacted the numbers as the annual rate of core inflation slow to 2.78%oya in the ECB’s seasonally adjusted data and was effectively stable at 2.86%oya in Eurostat’s non-seasonally adjusted data. Combined with the Italian effect, core inflation may have been close to our forecast of 2.70%oya.

Figure 4: Euro area core HICP prices
![](images/84a1177eaf7b94846d626d2cfa590b10a39c6275d866bcbf59839c444837f426.jpg)

Of course, it is not certain that the two factors cited above explain the upside surprise to core inflation. On the calculation effect, we note that the annual rate based on the ECB data have often led the Eurostat measure when divergences have opened up. But, this is not clear-cut and therefore the flash report for August will be important ahead of the ECB’s September meeting. For now, the trend in core goods inflation remains quite modest, despite the upside surprise in July, while momentum in services has come off its high but is still high (Figure 5). Hence, the ECB will want to see further progress over time. But, in an environment of weaker growth, we think that enough progress has been made to make a September cut very certain.

Figure 5: Euro area core inflation

Week of August 5 - 9

Output and surveys

Purchasing managers index final
<table><tr><td colspan="2"></td><td colspan="2"></td><td>Flash</td><td>Final</td></tr><tr><td>Mon</td><td>Euro area</td><td>May</td><td>Jun</td><td>Jul</td><td>Jul</td></tr><tr><td>Aug 5</td><td>Services - activity</td><td>53.2</td><td>52.8</td><td>51.9</td><td>51.9</td></tr><tr><td>10:00am</td><td>Composite - output</td><td>52.2</td><td>50.9</td><td>50.1</td><td>50.1</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Composite - employment</td><td>52.0</td><td>50.9</td><td>50.0</td><td>50.0</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Composite - new orders</td><td>51.6</td><td>49.4</td><td>49.2</td><td>49.2</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Composite - future output</td><td>63.1</td><td>60.8</td><td>60.0</td><td>60.0</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Composite - input prices</td><td>57.5</td><td>57.2</td><td>58.1</td><td>58.1</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Composite - output prices</td><td>52.6</td><td>52.4</td><td>52.2</td><td>52.2</td></tr></table>

The flash report showed another large decline in the PMI in July, taking its signal for GDP growth down from 1.5%ar in May to below 1%ar in July. The final PMI report for manufacturing showed a modest upward revisions, but one that still left it weak overall. This implies however that the final composite PMI could also see an upward revision. If not, it would show an economy that is losing momentum in a relatively broad-based way, even if services and employment would be holding up relatively well. Unfortunately, the exact reasons for the recent relapse are not clear-cut.

German orders have continued to slide in recent months, including on an ex-bulk basis. This has been consistent with the signal from the new orders index of the German manufacturing PMI, which points to around a 5% annualized pace of contraction. At the same time, the official data show the backlog of orders still at elevated levels. For June, we have pencilled in a continuation of this downward trend in new orders, broadly in line with the signal from the surveys. It will also be interesting to see if the weakness remains most pronounced in terms of domestic orders, which could be consistent with the sense that domestic policy uncertainties are one factor behind the weakness.

<table><tr><td></td><td></td><td>Mar</td><td>Apr</td><td>May</td><td>Jun</td></tr><tr><td>Thu</td><td>Germany Industry incl constr (%m/m,</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Aug 8</td><td>sa)</td><td>-0.4</td><td>0.1</td><td>-2.5</td><td>1.5</td></tr><tr><td>8:00am</td><td>%oya, sa</td><td>-4.2</td><td>-3.7</td><td>-6.7</td><td></td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Industry ex constr (%m/m, sa)</td><td>-0.3</td><td>0.4</td><td>-2.4</td><td>1.7</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Manufacturing (%m/m, sa)</td><td>-0.2</td><td>0.5</td><td>-2.9</td><td>2.0</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Construction (%m/m, sa)</td><td>-1.0</td><td>-1.2</td><td>-3.3</td><td>0.0</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Energy (%m/m, sa)</td><td>-1.3</td><td>-0.1</td><td>2.5</td><td>1.0</td></tr></table>

German IP was very weak in May. There was further payback in construction after mild weather had boosted output in the winter and there was also a large decline in manufacturing. We suspect that the timing of holidays impacted this, which would be consistent with the VDA data that showed car production slumping in May and recovering in June. One source of uncertainty is possible disruption from flooding in parts of Germany, but we suspect that any production impact was likely made up later in the month. Overall, our forecast would still leave IP down in 2Q24, but by less than signalled by the business surveys and also possibly by less than implied by the 2Q24 flash GDP report.

## Demand and labor markets

<table><tr><td>Retail sales</td><td>Mar</td><td>Apr</td><td>May</td><td>Jun</td></tr><tr><td>Tue Euro area</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Aug 6</td><td>Sales ex. autos and petroleum, volumes, sa</td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>11:00am</td><td>%m/m</td><td>0.7 -0.2</td><td>0.1</td><td>0.0</td></tr><tr><td>%oya</td><td></td><td>0.6 0.6</td><td>0.3</td><td></td></tr></table>

We expect Euro area retail sales to have been stable in June, which would leave 2Q24 up modestly by around 1%q/q saar and the data with a modest upward trend. For the latter, it would however be important to see renewed outright increases as we move into 2Q24. One source of uncertainty is the German data have been delayed until later in August by IT issues at the German statistics office, which means that neither May nor June are available for now.

From a broader perspective, the household saving rate increased significantly in 1Q24, against a backdrop of very strong income gains, and consumer confidence has continued to recover through July. Hence, there remains a decent case for consumer spending to step up, but the data are still failing to show strong growth.

The EC survey showed a fall in business confidence offsetting a rise in consumer confidence and a dramatic divergence in capacity utilization in services and manufacturing. But, it also showed labour shortages at still high levels even in manufacturing, which suggests ongoing support to hiring. The improvement in consumer confidence reflects a recovery in views about personal finances, higher major purchase intentions and, separately, there is also solidity in households’ views about the labour market.

The French economy expanded 1.1%q/q saar in 2Q24, in line with our forecast and slightly above consensus. This is a decent outcome that is modestly above the signal from the PMI. Domestic final sales contributed 0.5%-pt, net exports contributed 0.8%-pt and inventory changes detracted 0.1%-pt. On the domestic side, the changes were mostly small with household spending up just 0.2%q/q saar, capex up just 0.3%q/q saar and government consumption up a larger 1.2%q/q saar. The net trade contribution resulted from a solid 2.5%q/q saar gain in exports, while imports were stable. On the production side of GDP, declines in manufacturing and construction of around 4%q/q saar were more than offset by a 1.4%q/q saar increase in services (and with private services up 2%q/q saar). Overall, there is not a lot of momentum in many GDP components, but modest growth in various parts is still adding up to a trend-like outcome for the whole economy.

<table><tr><td></td><td>4Q23</td><td colspan="2">1Q24</td><td colspan="2">2Q24</td></tr><tr><td>Spain (flash estimate)</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>%q/q, sa</td><td>0.7</td><td></td><td>0.8</td><td></td><td>0.8</td></tr><tr><td>%q/q, saar</td><td>2.7</td><td>2.9</td><td>3.2</td><td>3.4 30</td><td>3.3</td></tr><tr><td>%oya</td><td>21</td><td>2.2</td><td>25</td><td>2.6</td><td>2.9</td></tr><tr><td>GDP components (%q/q saar)</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Private consumption</td><td>1</td><td>1.2</td><td>15</td><td>1.6</td><td>1.3</td></tr><tr><td>Government consumption</td><td>4.2</td><td>4.3</td><td>-2.5</td><td></td><td>1.0</td></tr><tr><td>Fixed capital formation</td><td>-6.4</td><td></td><td>10.7</td><td>10.8</td><td>3.6</td></tr><tr><td>Exports</td><td>116</td><td>11.7</td><td>13.6</td><td>13.8</td><td>4.8</td></tr><tr><td>Imports</td><td>10.0</td><td>9.9</td><td>9.0</td><td>8.9</td><td>-0.7</td></tr></table>

Spanish real GDP increased 3.3%q/q saar in 2Q23, in line with the growth seen in the prior two quarters. Half of this came from domestic final sales and the rest largely from net trade, which reflected a near 5%q/q saar jump in exports and a modest decline in imports. On the production side, the growth was broad based, with value added up 4.5%q/q saar in manufacturing and 4.5%q/q saar in private services. These outcomes are all solid and consistent with the recent strength of the Spanish PMI.

In terms of GDP prices, the GDP deflator barely increased (0.2%q/q saar), which left it at 3.2%oya and hence at half of its peak. The details show unit labour costs falling 1.6%q/q saar in 2Q24, reflecting both a decline in compensation per employee and a recovery in productivity per worker. The details of the GDP deflator also show unit profits increasing only slightly in 2Q24, while unit taxes rose by almost 10%q/q saar. While these details are noisy, gradual progress is being made in the disinflation journey.

Overall, the Spanish economy continues to perform very strongly, with broad-based strength across many components and helped by decent supply-side performance. In particular, delivered potential has been growing at around a 2.5% ar. Our forecast has a slowing in real GDP growth to 2%q/q saar in 3Q24 and 1.5%q/q saar in 4Q24, but risks are likely still skewed to the upside unless the final July PMI reveals a significant drop in Spain.

Of course, French GDP has already been released for 2Q24 and it is not clear that this is set for a revision due to the IP report. But, softness in IP would chime with the weakness in the French manufacturing PMI.

## Japan

• BoJ hikes to 0.25%, announces QT plan—in line with our long-standing call

• Ueda provides concrete guidance that further rate hikes will follow at a gradual pace

• Inflation, wage outlook tilted to the upside

BoJ looks comfortable with the consumption trend; upgrading GDP forecasts amid spending upturn

The BoJ policy meeting this week will likely prove to have been one of the most significant of the cycle. The BoJ hiked the policy rate and announced its QT plan, in line with our long-standing but out-of-consensus call. Perhaps more importantly, in stating that more hikes will be delivered if the data flow comes in as expected, Ueda gave his strongest signal to date that the runway for further policy normalization will be a long one.

The policy statement was a clear hawkish break—our NLP model puts it on par with some of the most hawkish on record for the BoJ. It offered direct guidance that policy accommodation will continue to be scaled back. And, in suggesting that further hikes would bring the BoJ closer to neutral, we think that Ueda has signaled in concrete terms that the terminal rate will be higher than the 0.5% level which is (barely) priced by markets over the next year (Figure 1).

Figure 1: NLP score of BoJ policy statement and 1Y1M OIS
![](images/fd1d011213e9c54db862e27bf90a0649595dd70907f6e5ec620f7248b0bb764c.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan. Dashed line shows July statement.

The BoJ thus delivered a set of policy outcomes and guidance that was in line with our call: we continue to see the policy rate at 1% by end-2025, with the next hike penciled in for December 2024. But the BoJ’s hawkish turn seems to have caught some off guard, given what has been a patchy run for macro activity over the past several quarters, particularly in terms of soft household spending.

Focusing on the weak run in consumption risks missing the forest for the trees, in our view. The BoJ’s more medium-term view looks past this consumption pot-hole to focus on the interaction of higher wage growth with underlying inflation, with the Bank suggesting that rising wages will support spending even in the face of higher prices. We agree with the Bank’s view that inflation risks are tilted to the upside. This assessment, we think, should continue to underpin a gradual rise in the policy rate.

Against this backdrop, the incoming activity data suggest that a recovery in domestic activity is also taking hold. June retail sales and July consumer sentiment reports were positive, and manufacturers’ projections into 3Q were nudged up. We are upgrading our 2Q GDP real consumption estimate to 2%q/q saar from 0.5% in the prior forecast, and now see GDP growth holding at an above-trend 2.0%-2.2%q/q saar pace through mid-year.

## BoJ delivers hawkish wake-up call

There were two dissenting votes which called for a postponement of the past week’s rate hike to 0.25%. There were no dissenting votes on the QT decision, and the BoJ decided to reduce its monthly JGB purchases by JPY0.4 trillion each quarter from the current JPY5.7trillion, reaching JPY2.9 trillion by 1Q26. This was broadly in line with market expectations.

The policy statement was hawkish compared with market expectations. The BoJ clearly stated the possibility of additional rate hikes and scaling back the degree of monetary accommodation, if the outlook for economic activity and prices presented in the BoJ’s Outlook Report is realized. The BoJ assessed private consumption to have been resilient, and sees upside risk to inflation towards next year with broadening wage momentum, elevated inflation expectations, and a rise in import prices. In the outlook report, the BoJ revised down FY24 growth and inflation forecasts, while raising FY25 inflation forecasts. But these revisions were due to the recent data revisions and policy decisions, and the BoJ didn’t see them as factors undermining positive prospects, as we expected. In the policy statement, the BoJ clearly stated its plan to continue raising the policy rate towards neutral, along with its rising confidence in achieving its policy goal.

At the press conference, Governor Ueda echoed the policy statement, sending a clear message that the BoJ likely will continue to deliver rate hikes at a gradual pace. He repeated the BoJ’s assessment that the current monetary policy settings are very accommodative even after this week’s decision. He even stated that the policy settings will likely remain accommodative even after a few more rate hikes, mentioning that the policy rate of 0.5%, which the market is focusing on, is not the level that the BoJ sees as a limit. We think this is a clear message from the BoJ that it plans to continue scaling back policy accommodation so that it can bring the policy rate back to neutral by the time the BoJ gains full confidence in achieving the target. With this strategy, the BoJ appears to think it can reduce the risk of rapid rate hikes in the future. While repeating the data dependency on wages and cost pass through, he didn’t exclude the possibility of an additional rate hike by end-2024.

Last week, we argued that this week’s decision would be a touchstone of the BoJ’s policy reaction function to test whether the BoJ will make policy decisions based on an assessment of the economy and inflation in a forward-looking manner. The decision showed that policy decisions from here will be based on economic fundamentals. We think this means the BoJ is likely to leave forecasts of the timing of additional rate hikes to market participants rather than providing clear preset guidance through media leaks. We think this is part of the BoJ’s efforts to restore market functionality. As we see rising upside risks to inflation backed by strong wage momentum, we continue to expect the BoJ to hike the policy rate to 0.5% in December this year, followed by two more hikes in 2025 to bring the policy rate to 1% by end-2025.

## Revising up consumption into 3Q

June retail sales and July consumer sentiment pointed to a gradual recovery in domestic consumption, as we have been projecting. June retail sales rose at a weaker-than-expected 0.6%m/m sa, but the underlying trend still rose for the third consecutive month, increasing to 7.5%q/q saar, the strongest pace since 3Q23. Details suggest signs of a broad recovery; motor vehicle sales further rose 4.0%m/m sa after a surge in May, and spending on apparel, food and electronics reversed a drop in the prior month.

Figure 2: Japan Retail sales and consumer sentiment
![](images/09f2e1e3ece882b9e26a6181ac1da6c1685f7a0ff7a4b2277420b1c56a23a729.jpg)

Figure 3: Retail sales and BoJ Consumption Activity Index (CAI)
![](images/7f070be010a9ffc28067728d3aae5f77b41607bbaf82e9ff8d27dcec0e4ad62c.jpg)

July consumer sentiment rose modestly by 0.3pt to 36.7, its second monthly increase in a row. The level is still lower than April, but sub-indices showed households’ broad improvement in sentiment. Indices for the standard of living rose 0.7pt to 34.5; timing to buy durable goods rose 0.4pt to 30.0; and labor market conditions increased 0.3pt to 42.0. Consumers’ inflation expectations remain elevated, although they fell slightly in July, possibly because of the re-introduction of electricity subsidies.

While consumers are facing sticky inflation, we think higher wages and the government’s one-off tax rebate are supporting momentum in spending this quarter (Figures 2 and 3). The extension of electricity/gas subsidies should also mitigate concerns about rising inflation in the summer, encouraging the trend recovery in spending to continue.

BoJ’s Outlook Report, released at this week’s policy meeting, also assessed that domestic consumption has been firm recently, which is consistent with today’s surveys. Against the backdrop of nominal spending increases in the monthly retail sales data, we are revising our 2Q GDP real consumption forecast upwards to 2%q/q saar from 0.5%.

## Raising our sights on the industrial cycle

Japan’s June industrial production fell 3.6%m/m sa, a smaller drop than our forecast and consensus expectations, both of which were for a 4.5% contraction. Despite the decline, the sequential trend rose 12.2%3m/3m saar, its fastest pace since end-2022, reflecting strength in prior months. As expected, the bulk of the fall in output last month owed to the impact of auto production suspension at several large manufacturers, reflecting the impact of the second wave of safety testing scandals.

Figure 4: Japan Manufacturing output %3m/3m saar, dashed line is manufacturers' projection
![](images/fc4dabd429e6abdd141b4f2815a0d33445c54454f679bde620ac2cc46c805d35.jpg)

But producers look to be turning more optimistic that these drags are fading through the summer. Projections for July output were revised up to 6.5%m/m sa from 3.6% the prior month, and manufacturers expect a further 0.7% rise in August. If realized, these projections should leave manufacturing expanding through 3Q, albeit at a slower pace than 2Q (Figure 4).

Auto output dropped 8.9%m/m sa, reversing around half of the 18.1% pop in the prior month. Momentum in auto output has been choppy given the start-stop trend in production through 1H24. But in level terms, car output has not yet recovered to its pre-pandemic level, potentially suggesting some loss of global market share over the past several years. Electronics production was also weak, falling 5.8%m/m sa, though in contrast to the auto sector, chip output remains much higher than its pre-pandemic trend, reflecting the impact of new production facilities in Japan (Figures 5 and 6).

Figure 5: Japan Manufacturing output by sector 2013=100, sa
![](images/8d04fd28661d9444ef5d5d19983a10fdfe93d2fec023d0080e0c27c8ce8fd655.jpg)
Source: METI, J.P. Morgan

Figure 6: Japan Electronic parts and devices output
![](images/928628c8f3197e7d3cafc67b2374c4eb4cbbb0a58dd98c7e581ea86bb31dfce9.jpg)

## Review of past week’s data

Labor force survey (Jul 30)

<table><tr><td></td><td>Apr</td><td>May</td><td>Jun</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Unemployment rate (% sa)</td><td>2.6</td><td>2.6</td><td>26</td><td>2.5</td></tr><tr><td>Job offers ratio (% sa)</td><td>1.26</td><td>1.24</td><td>124</td><td>1.23</td></tr></table>

The job offers ratio fell for third consecutive month, decreasing to 1.23% sa, the lowest level since April 2022. The decline likely reflects the temporary production halt in the auto sector; job offers from manufactures fell 14.6%oya. On the other hand, the unemployment rate improved, falling for the first time in five months by 10bp to 2.5%. These results imply that the labor demand drop is limited to some SMEs that are struggling due to higher input costs.

## Canada

• We expect next Friday’s Labor Force Survey to show moderate employment growth in July...

• ...but we think the unemployment rate will tick up another tenth to 6.5%

The BoC easing already underway should help cushion the labor market

Next week’s June trade data will likely continue to suggest net exports were a drag on real GDP growth last quarter

Data on the labor market south of the border disappointed notably this week, with monthly payroll growth decelerating close to a cycle low and the unemployment rate unexpectedly jumping two-tenths to 4.3%. Weather may have been a modest drag on the data (despite the Bureau of Labor Statistics suggesting otherwise), but US markets dropped appreciably in response to an overall message that points to a weakening labor market. But a low-4% unemployment rate looks positively benign to Canadian workers, especially to new entrants to the labor force from schooling or immigration who have been finding it increasingly difficult to get hired. Unfortunately, we see only limited near-term relief as we look for the unemployment rate to tick up further in July.

Figure 1: Unemployment rate
![](images/c7fbbc41787e8dc10644b873534be17b69c69287b082b5155b94b01a454e0b8d.jpg)

Canada has seen a sizable 1.6%-point rise in the unemployment rate over the past two years (Figure 1), about twice as large as the cumulative increase in the US. While a move of this size might be associated with a recession in the US — and is certainly of concern in Canada — the broader economy has avoided a downturn thus far, in part because the rise in the unemployment rate appears to be largely due to an even faster growth in population and labor force growth of around 3% annually, which is yet to show any sustained indication of slowing to its below 2% pre-pandemic trend. The rise in the unemployment rate also looks to have some flavor of a normalization after reaching a multi-decade low early in the pandemic recovery. The Bank of Canada’s recent dovish pivot reflects rising concern about the health of the labor market as inflation has moved back within the target band, and the earlier start to easing by the BoC already underway — in contrast to the more dug-in Fed — should help cushion the labor market further. While we aren’t looking for a recession, we also aren’t convinced the economy is out of the woods yet, and look for continued steady easing by the BoC into next year.

## Expecting moderate growth in July jobs

We expect next Friday’s Labor Force Survey to show employment rising 20k in July after slowing to a near-stall in June. Job growth had been running strong—just below a 2% annual pace in 1H24—before the June data showed a significant loss of momentum at midyear. Job gains have not been able to keep pace with population growth thanks in large part to strong immigration (Figure 2). As a result, the unemployment rate has been trending steadily higher since the start of 2023, and we look for it to tick up another tenth next week to 6.5%. The annual pace of average hourly earnings for permanent employees should drop back below 5%oya in July after surprising to the upside in June, consistent with the easing in firms’ wage growth expectations seen in the latest Business Outlook Survey from the Bank of Canada.

Figure 2: Employment and working age population growth %oya
![](images/b495d4acf9c5a8a5031e2a673224693e30a0160cca1af93dfe69bde7a4f759cd.jpg)

## No safety net

Next Tuesday’s June merchandise trade report from Statistics Canada will round out the full set of monthly trade figures for 2Q24. After the nominal merchandise trade deficit widened to C\$1.9bn in May, our estimates suggest that a rebound in imports (+1.8%) outpaced a rebound exports (+1.1%) last month. This would leave the deficit at C\$2.4bn in June. Net exports are already tracking a considerable drag on real GDP growth last quarter, which has kept our expenditure-based tracking of 2Q24 real GDP growth well below Statistics Canada’s output-based estimate, which is running around 2%ar for the second quarter. While the trade data have been implying downside risk to GDP growth, it is important to note that there is a fair bit of volatility in the trade data, and we have limited visibility on the services side of the trade balance.

## Mexico

• Growth moderation confirmed in 2Q as broad-based disappointment results in 0.9%saar

• We now see economic activity expanding by a belowpotential 1.5% this year

Banxico to cut 25bp on both downside risks to growth and disinflation gains in core gauges

• Next week, July CPI and June IP also in focus

According to the Flash Report released this week by the INE-GI, economic activity expanded timidly last quarter (0.2%saqr), resulting in a lukewarm annualized print of 0.9%saar, well below our expectations for 2% (and the consensus’ 1.6%). Our forecasts implied June growth at 0.6%, but now it looks like the end-of-quarter pace will be instead a modest contraction (-0.1samr) that will also set the stage for a modest path in 2H24. We now expect full-year growth at 1.5% down from 1.8% before (Figure 1).

While the bulk of this week's result can be explained by a 1.7%q/q contraction in agricultural activity (-6.8%saar) mostly explained by wild swings in weather conditions, both industrial production and services expanded timidly (0.3%saqr and 0.26%, respectively), challenging expectations for a trend-like performance this year. The first half of 2024 was originally expected to reflect the revival of manufacturing production on the back of improving external demand and the sustained momentum in domestic consumption on the heels of government spending ahead of the election.

Figure 1: Annual and quarterly GDP outlook
![](images/67998018c62a28d44f11c6b094f960fb3b7e134b69ab8337148c3e6e5e402501.jpg)
Source: INEGI and J.P. Morgan forecasts. \*Dotted line: annual average (%oya labeled); bars: observed seasonally adjusted annual rates (%q/q, saar)

In the end, disappointing dynamics in both categories suggest the economy is facing significant challenges that reflect (1) capacity/infrastructure constraints and short-term restrictive policies and (2) poor policy planning and slow execution of infrastructure projects. We expect government spending to regain some of its footing in 2H24 on the back of the overdue projects (e.g., Maya train completion, refining production, among others) and along with private consumption should

result in a mild rebound in 2H24 as compared to 1H (1.4% vs 1%).

Going into the next few quarters, the ongoing deterioration in the domestic business outlook indicators in an uncertain geopolitical context, along with an already visible moderation in formal employment and a tight 2025 budget are expected to dent growth and to channel through lower domestic and foreign investment.

Consumption will remain firm on increasing discretionary spending, but some moderation is also expected. Still, if external demand gains traction on the back of improving growth in the US and a weaker MXN, we could revisit our earlier (more bullish) call for a manufacturing rebound. For now, we eye growth for 2025 at 1.2%, one-tenth below our earlier estimation.

The June IP report could offer some soothing news amid this week’s downbeat early GDP release. The manufacturing sector seems likely to deliver a solid 0.8%m/m, sa gain as it continues to alternate the fits and starts that have characterized the long past two years when it has moved sideways. This should be enough to offset weakness in construction (-0.7%m/m, sa) and mining (-0.6%). Altogether, we’d expect IP to eke out a 0.3%m/m gain, stronger vs. the two-tenth decline implicit in the 2Q GDP early release. At face value, this would leave IP up 1.9%ar in 2Q instead of the 1.2% initially reported.

## Banxico to deliver a neutral cut

Next week, we expect the COPOM to cut rates by 25bp to 10.75% in a divided 3-2 decision. But the decision is not expected to be straightforward and should keep Banxico on edge even if now we expect a rather neutral statement.

Incoming information in recent weeks has complicated Banxico’s guidance, particularly on the inflation front. Non-core pressures related to scattered spikes in raw food prices and upside pressures emanating from the unwinding of energy subsidies drove inflation to 5.6% in the first half of July— which suggests the staff should be revising up its headline inflation path. In June, the board expected inflation to average 4.5% for 3Q but according to our estimates, it is now tracking 5.1%. Also, for 4Q, we now expect 4.6%. These arguments, in our view, will be enough to vindicate hawkish members of the board.

However, we believe the arguments in favor of a cut are strong enough to continue forecasting gradual actions of 25bp, and more so after the Fed’s dovish pivot this week. Core inflation remains on a steady downtrend, which has been supportive of further corrections in expectations—in fact, Banxico will probably revise the core path down. A second important factor is on the growth front, and the Board was careful enough to signal downside risks to economic activity back in June. Fears have been confirmed ever since.

Still, for us, the most important arguments are the degree of policy tightening and now, the Fed factor. With the ex-ante policy rate above 7% and with the Fed now expected to cut back-to-back from September according to our US team (Figure 2), we strongly believe the bank will seize the opportunity to gain traction in the normalization process considering that the road to neutral levels (around 7%) is still long and winding. We will revisit our path after the statement next week.

Figure 2: Banxico vs. Fed policy rate outlook
![](images/465255d27010a7026013dd9cd7e0aeec153764ba714879bf47b758367a8d72e9.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan forecasts. Updated June 27 (Banxico) and August 2 (FOMC)

## Easing non-core fears in late July

Next week, the final July inflation report will be released. Volatility in the non-core component should continue to drive gyrations in headline inflation in the 2H of July, this time pulling the over-year-ago inflation reading sharply lower to 5.37% from 5.61% as agricultural prices drop and high base effects compound the impact of such decline.

We warned, however, about reading too much into non-core driven shifts in headline inflation and focused instead on the signal out of core inflation, and here news should remain benign.

Figure 3: Quarterly Core inflation path
![](images/37b0eec3eb4b182af9bddd7e361c6a43c35347f6c3bbc1c276f0d00cc188dc9f.jpg)
Source: INEGI, Banxico and J.P. Morgan forecasts. Last update for Banxico Mar June 27, 2024

We look for core inflation to drop through the upper bound of Banxico’s tolerance band (4%) for the first time since early 2021 (Figure 3) reaching 3.96% in the 2H of July. On a 3m3m annualized basis we expect core inflation to cool to 3.4% (sa), further stressing the loss of momentum in price gains in this component.

## Brazil

BCB’s inflation forecast remained close to the target at the monetary policy horizon

• But BCB sent more hawkish messages in face of rising inflation expectations and FX depreciation

• The fiscal deficit remains persistently large

Economic growth looks robust with a tight labor market in 2Q

Contrary to the global economy, which seems primed to enter the most synchronized easing cycle outside of a recession, the attention in this week’s COPOM meeting was on whether the central bank would indicate hikes in the near term, as the market is currently pricing. BCB kept the policy rate stable at 10.50%, as broadly expected, while the statement was balanced with a dovish CPI forecast at the monetary horizon and a hawkish guidance in its wording (Figure 1).

Figure 1: Nominal and real (ex-ante) SELIC rate
![](images/2e17fa13990dcc412355da110a6a674c98030118510f110dca2924e97db2aef7.jpg)

The central bank’s inflation projection for its main target— one year and a half ahead—were benign. Indeed, the central bank raised its 2024 and 2025 CPI forecasts to 4.2% (from 4.0%) and to 3.4% (from 3.1%), respectively, close to expectations. But the Board chose to anticipate the recent modifications of the revised inflation targeting framework, clearly shifting the focus to inflation in 1Q26. The forecast for this period was at 3.2%, just two-tenths above the target, assuming stable SELIC and BRL at 5.55.

BCB also introduced a few hawkish tweaks to the language. The central bank is concerned about the inflationary impacts of the asset prices deterioration and rising inflation expectations, which—if persistent—corroborate the need for more vigilance. Also, the balance of risks introduced the upside risk of the inflationary impact, possibly through a more depreciated exchange rate, of domestic and external economic policies. Those concerns, combined with resilient GDP growth, BCB’s higher inflation forecasts, and de-anchored inflation expectations require “even more” caution and diligence, according to BCB’s statement.

All considered, we keep our expectation for the policy rate at 10.50% ahead, reckoning that the guidance ambiguity should be clarified in next week’s meeting minutes. On Tuesday it will be particularly important to understand the central bank’s assessment of the balance of risks, which now has three upside risks and two downside risks. The policy discussion and eventual divergences in the Board will likely clarify the conditions necessary for an eventual policy rate move this year.

In our view, if financial assets continue to deteriorate it will be difficult to avoid a hike, in particular if it is driven by an additional loss of credibility over domestic fiscal policy. The fact that the spending freeze enacted recently limits how expenses can be appropriated until late-September helps, but in that sense we see the 2025 budget proposal in August 31 as the key fiscal event to monitor between now and then.

## Persistent fiscal deficit

The central government posted a BRL40.9bn primary deficit in June, slightly worse than our forecast for BRL40bn deficit and consensus BRL39.5bn estimate. The central government’s imbalance between growth for revenues and expenditures, at 8.5% and 10.5% ytd in real terms, remained the main driver behind the 2.4% of GDP deficit in the last 12 months.

With a considerably larger interest rate bill, further pushed by marginal losses on FX swaps, financial expenditures reached BRL835.7bn in 12 months. Nominal results deteriorated three-tenths to 9.9% of GDP, the highest in over three years, while gross and net public debt increased to 77.8% and 62.1% of GDP, respectively (Figure 2).

Figure 2: Fiscal balance
![](images/26fdef794e49a4d6341b3ed228c44188c992abe70df47ea71dfcecee3cc250a7.jpg)
With markets’ growing impatience it will be important to deliver a 2025 budget that is consistent with the targets not only at the headline level, but that withstands the in-depth scrutiny that will surely follow.

Fiscal sector

## Industrial production ends 2Q with a boom

IP ended 2Q with the strongest monthly expansion since July 2020, growing 4.1%m/m, sa (Figure 3). The story behind the headline was different from previous months. While mining continued to expand, manufacturing not only improved, but also surpassed the level seen before RS floods. This result pushed our 2Q nowcaster from 3.3% to 4.3%, significantly above our call for a 2.5% expansion. With sentiment indicators, which remained robust over 2Q, kicking off 3Q at even higher levels, we remain encouraged about the sector performance over coming months.

Figure 3: Industrial Production
![](images/b872f5ef09bfa24f02da54b74793861619cf2168cc3d660998c0aecee43c4679.jpg)
Jan 22 Apr 22 Jul 22 Oct 22 Jan 23 Apr 23 Jul 23 Oct 23 Jan 24 Apr 24 Source: IBGE and J.P Morgan

## Labor market remains tight

The June unemployment rate was released at 6.9%, matching the consensus estimate but a tenth above our call, keeping the seasonally adjusted rate at 6.9%,. A few measures of labor market tightness, such as the layoff ratio, quits rate, and hiring rate, moderated in the month. However, almost all of them remain historically high and our firm-side unemployment rate is at all-time lows. The deviation between different wage data remains striking. The usually received real wages grew 0.6%m/m, sa, accelerating two-tenths to 5.8%oya, as a result of nominal wages growing 10%. Even just the formal job wage growth, reported as growing close to 8%oya, looks inconsistent with the formal labor market report (CAGED) and the FIPE wage data that are around 5% (Figure 4).
