## Argentina

The cash-basis current account posted -US\$0.2bn in June, the first month in the red since October 2023

On a seasonally adjusted basis, lower exports explained the deterioration

• Commercial debt in 2Q24 would have been reduced to about US\$2.4bn

• In our estimates, net FX reserves stood at -US\$4.7bn as of July 23, from -US\$1.1bn by June-end

• We update the net reserves trajectory through yearend, based on recent policy announcements

This week the government reinforced its commitment to zero monetary issuance, and MinFin Caputo anticipated in a meeting with market participants that they expect inflation to print around a 1% monthly level already by September (well below our expectation and monthly consensus). President of BCRA Bausili explained that July’s decline in gross reserves was driven by both higher debt payments and energy imports, and anticipated that in August they expect this trend to reverse amid a growing energy surplus. Moreover, the authorities announced new multilateral loans increasing reserves in early August, including US\$647.5mn from the IADB “to support public finances and BoP needs”. Last, MinFin Caputo emphasized that they are negotiating repo loans with banks to secure bond amortizations for the next year and a half (note there are US\$5.8bn in market debt principal payments in 2025). In all, the authorities seek to drive inflation expectations lower, while reinforcing willingness and capacity to service external debt ahead, so as to appease market concerns. On this regard, after a red July, the reserves trajectory ahead will be key to assess the ability of the Milei administration to build a bridge until the full release of capital controls and the transition into a new (sustainable) policy framework, as we expand below.

## A (cash-basis) monthly deficit in June

The cash-basis current account posted a US\$0.2bn deficit in June, the first month in red since October 2023. The monthly deterioration was driven by the merchandise trade balance. On a seasonally adjusted basis, the merchandise trade balance printed at US\$1.3bn in June compared to US\$2.4bn 3mma through May. Such deterioration was explained by lower export inflows as import payments remained relatively flat on the month when adjusting by seasonality (yet up 30% from 1Q24 average). Of note, the US\$393mn increase in imports (nsa) is mostly related to the payment of energy imports on the back of the winter season.

The good news is that the YTD cash-basis current account still posted a sizable US\$7.5bn surplus, a strong reversal compared to the US\$5.8bn deficit observed in 1H23.

As highlighted in prior research the YTD reversal has been explained by a US\$15bn decline in import payments, while export inflows decreased US\$2bn. Of note, the export underperformance, both on a monthly basis and YTD, comes to be explained by the blend FX arrangement, in which 20% of total exports (US\$7.6bn YTD) are being supplied to financial FX, so are not counted as cash exports for the MULC report. This compounds the below historical average inflows from the cereal complex (-21% in June and -9% YTD) as well as the deceleration in export inflows from the rest of the sectors, of which the REER appreciation may have a say (particularly in the auto sector).

On the imports side, the BCRA announced that starting August 1 import payments can be made in two installments (vs. four previously), further normalizing the imports payment chain. Note the accrued vs. cash basis data shows US\$2.6bn in importers’ commercial debt accumulation in 2Q24. That said, the BCRA noted in the latest external debt report that US\$5bn in imports were paid through the bluechip swap market in 2Q24, with another US\$4.3bn in 1Q (Figure 1). When considering imports paid both through the official and the parallel FX markets, the gap between the accrued and cash basis imports would have narrowed by about US\$2.4bn in 2Q24, reversing most of the commercial debt accumulation seen in the prior quarter (US\$2.7bn).

Figure 1: Imports payments framework and commercial debt
![](images/e336cc4edbb11a25061c41b1a67123f3782b42ad8f5e2a5a7eb855bdbee8536d.jpg)
Source: BCRA and J.P. Morgan

Regarding the services balance, note that after increasing the prior month, tourism outflows remained relatively stable in June, standing around the 2023 monthly average, though still well below the record levels observed in 2018.

Also worth highlighting on the financing side, it seems that the loans inflows novelty of March and April (see note) was interrupted in June with US\$136mn outflows under this concept. This was more than offset by US\$622mn in multilateral inflows in June, which allowed for a US\$358mn increase in gross international reserves on the month (US\$5.9bn YTD), despite the current account shortfall recorded on the month.

## Updating net reserves trajectory

The authorities recently announced the transition into Phase II of the policy framework, in which they aim to achieve zero monetary issuance. Against this backdrop, they unveiled their plan to sterilize ARS2.5tn (equivalent to around US\$1.9bn) via selling hard currency in the parallel FX market. The government acknowledged the temporary nature of this arrangement as it only works under capital controls and the objective is to liberalize the capital account when conditions permit. It was also noted that market conditions are to be assessed when determining whether or not to supply hard currency in the parallel market (i.e., BCRA’s own discretion in terms of the timing of intervention). After the announcement, the FX gap stabilized around 40%, from 50% average in the prior week (Figure 2).

Despite the modest accumulation seen in gross international reserves in June, the BCRA data through July 26 show gross reserves down by US\$1.7bn. This is explained by US\$2.6bn in debt service to the bondholders and US\$714mn in payments to IMF, partially offset by US\$141mn purchases in the official FX market and US\$1.8bn in reserve requirements from USD deposits. Moreover, while we lack official data for FX intervention in the parallel FX market, the “other” category for reserves variation (which also includes, for example, changes in accounting differences for gold, SDRs, and yuan) suggests sales of around US\$206mn in the parallel FX market between July 15 and July 26 (US\$21mn on average per day).

Tax revenues
Figure 2: Reserves accumulation and FX gap
![](images/2799ba719b8d2f9e0561bb993d6a48548135084722b18ca417f5d13b96106302.jpg)

In our estimates, net FX reserves stood at -US\$4.7bn as of July 23, from -US\$1.1bn by June-end (our estimate considers as hard-currency liabilities Bopreal series 2 amortizations for US\$2.2bn in the next 12 months but not Treasury deposits). Meanwhile, USD liquid reserves stood at around US\$6bn (from almost null when the administration took office), when including the US\$1.5bn at a fiduciary account to service January 2025 interest payments.

Based on the recent announcement, we also update our assumptions of the BCRA net reserves accumulation for the remainder of 2024. Regarding external debt service, there is an additional US\$1.4bn (August-December) in interest payments to the IMF and another US\$3.8bn in debt service with other multilaterals (no market debt service until January next year, when it amounts to US\$4.6bn). Note we assume multilaterals (ex-IMF) debt service is fully financed in 2H24.

Moreover, we anticipate US\$1.5bn in parallel FX intervention through end-2024. That said, we also assume similar inflows into the parallel FX market due to the tax amnesty/RIGI (regime for large investments), which could help further narrow the FX gap and allow for the lifting of the 80/20 export blend scheme by 4Q24.

In all, based on these assumptions, we estimate net reserves declining by US\$3.7bn in 3Q24, then recovering by US\$1.3bn in the last quarter of the year. This is consistent with net reserves closing the year at around -US\$4bn, implying an accumulation of US\$7bn, in line with the IMF program target. We believe advancing toward the release of capital controls is paramount to secure and accelerate the ability to replenish reserves in the medium term.

The authors wish to thank Juan Goldin, of the Latin America Economics Research team, JPMorgan Chase Bank Sucursal Buenos Aires, for his contribution to this report.

## Andeans

Chile: The CBC unanimously decided to keep the policy rate stable at 5.75%

Our baseline forecast is for a 25bp cut in December, bringing the policy rate to 5.5% by year-end

Colombia: In a majority decision, the Board reduced the policy rate by 50bp, to 10.75%

Our base case is for an acceleration of the easing ahead, to 75bp in the last three meetings of the year

December ’24 policy rate is expected ar 8.5%; the terminal rate is maintained at 6.25%

## Chile: A (likely extended) pause

In line with our base case, the CBC unanimously decided to keep the policy rate stable at 5.75%. In terms of guidance, the Board estimates that if the assumptions in June’s macroeconomic central scenario are realized (see note), the policy rate would have accumulated most of the cuts planned for this year during its first half. This scenario also anticipates that the policy rate will continue to decrease over the two-year policy horizon. Our baseline forecast is for a 25bp cut in the December meeting, bringing the policy rate to 5.5% by yearend. We also maintain a terminal level of 4.75%.

In the statement, the Board emphasized that June inflation was in line with the CBC’s central scenario, while the core measure had a smaller-than-expected variation, mainly in goods, due to factors considered transitory (see note). On expectations, the statement does not discuss the recent move higher, but focuses on the 2-year policy horizon expectations, which are anchored around 3% (Figure 1). Economic activity is noted to have evolved below what was projected in the central scenario (see note).

Figure 1: Chile inflation expectations
![](images/005b61acc92dce183da63b96b72944ddf56090bbf26340cd3a6ba07bafa77054.jpg)
Source: CBC and J.P. Morgan

Regarding the evolution of financial variables, on the external front, it’s noted that global inflation has generally continued to decrease at a moderate pace, stressing the market’s outlook on the Federal Reserve’s somewhat less restrictive monetary policy. In the local financial market, short- and long-term interest rates have decreased since the last policy meeting, while the CLP depreciation aligns with the decline in copper prices.

## Colombia: Steady easing for now

In a majority decision, the Board opted to reduce the policy rate by 50bp, bringing it down to 10.75%. As in previous meetings, five directors voted in favor of the cut, while two directors advocated for a more substantial reduction of 75bp. The Board emphasized that future rate decisions will be contingent on new economic data.

In its statement, the Board noted that annual inflation in June remained stable at 7.2%oya, in line with expectations. That said, during the press conference, it was highlighted that core inflation performed better than anticipated by the staff, decreasing to 6.0%oya. Regarding inflation expectations, the monthly survey conducted by the central bank among analysts reported stable median inflation expectations, while those derived from the market fell across all maturities. On economic activity, it was noted that GDP likely continued its recovery path during 2Q. The technical team forecasts that economic activity levels will continue to recover, reaching 1.8%y/y growth in 2024, coming closer to our 2.0% projection. Finally, external financing conditions have become more accommodative due to expectations of more lenient monetary policies from the major central banks of advanced economies.

Figure 2: Colombia monetary policy rate
![](images/491d3f2b5516854e0ee96b35028552b85d9d93e00c479bafd6fa4ce4da1f276a.jpg)

The still high ex-ante real policy rate, the fact that core inflation declined a bit more quickly than the Staff expected, a close to null output gap, and the recent fiscal adjustment (though fiscal risks linger) align for additional easing ahead (Figure 2). These idiosyncratic factors compound the monetary easing expected from the Fed. Given this context, we anticipate an acceleration in monetary easing in the upcoming meetings. This expectation has been part of our baseline scenario since the beginning of the year. The main risk comes from the potential increase of diesel prices, which has not been decided yet, though the fiscal projections and FinMin

statement seem to suggest diesel could be increased in September/October.

Our base case projects that BanRep will reduce the policy rate by 75bp per meeting in the last three meetings of the year, aiming for a policy rate of 8.5% by December. The terminal rate is maintained at 6.25%.

The authors wish to thank Juan Goldin, of the Latin America Economics Research team, JPMorgan Chase Bank Sucursal Buenos Aires, for his contribution to this report.

## United Kingdom

BoE cut rates 25bps in August, in a finely balanced decision

• Forecasts appear consistent with a quarterly pace of further easing

• BoE hinted at the possibility of more, but is proceeding cautiously for now given the risks

Reeves highlights overspend and says taxes will need to rise

The BoE cut rates this week, although the vote was very close as we had expected. The BoE has a cautious easing bias, and we continue to see the next cut in November and a quarterly easing cycle. While the BoE did hint at potentially faster cuts as inflation risks fade, a growth recovery and still high underlying inflation suggest it is minded to stay cautious. Separately, the Chancellor this week responded to the spending audit she commissioned from the Treasury. Below, we lay out some estimates for what this means at the next Budget, including additional tax rises and spending cuts.

## More fiscal pressure

The spending audit (Table 1) revealed a larger-than-expected fiscal shortfall due to undeclared overspending in certain areas by the previous government. That reflects spending on rail maintenance, Ukrainian aid and Asylum costs, and leaves the public finances looking in a worse-than-expected situation. Some elements of the spending pressures identified by the review, however, are not a surprise. Notably, the need to increase public sector pay by close to 5.5%. As such, the Labour response to the audit does feel as though it is partly motivated by a means to justify tax rises at the next Budget. Labour had indicated it had no plans to do so prior to the election, but is slowly shifting its stance and leveraging off the audit. Reeves commented this week that taxes would have to be raised.

That said, Labour has continued to rule out raising the main tax rates. This reduces the scope for significant revenues to be raised, for example by increasing inheritance or capital gains tax. We were already working with the assumption that Labour would increase taxes by more than it had indicated prior to the election, and so the shift may not be as significant as it appears. Nevertheless, the news this week does suggest that: (a) a change from the stated plans will happen more quickly than expected, and (b) Labour will have to go further than we expected due to the genuine surprises in the audit.

Taking account of reserve funds and allowances made by the Treasury, the estimated £35bn gross shortfall works out to be closer to £22bn. But Reeves this week already committed to some spending cancellations and reductions in departmental budgets worth £5.5bn. That effectively takes the shortfall down to £16.4bn. Not all of this is permanent spending. The figure refers to 2024/25 and some elements will roll off naturally (e.g. foreign aid). This will show up as a larger fiscal drag next year, likely beyond the 0.6% shown in the current numbers. But there does appear to be a gap of around £10bn that needs to be plugged in order to prevent the more permanent elements resulting in a sustained rise in borrowing.

We think Labour could try to raise extra revenues in excess of £5bn at the next Budget, confirmed for 30<sup>th</sup> October. That would be in addition to measures already committed to prior to the election, which come close to £7bn. We think the remaining £5bn or so could be met by finding leeway in the fiscal rules. We don’t expect the definition of debt to be changed at this stage, but as the forecast rolls on a year we estimate this could free up another £10bn or so of space, given the way the rules are configured. We had previously expected Labour to use this to dial back planned cuts to the budgets of non-protected departments. But if more now needs to be earmarked to fund the surprise overspend, Labour may also have to accept a greater squeeze unprotected departmental budget allocations - assuming it does not raise taxes more significantly.

## BoE cuts rates, with cautious easing bias

The BoE cut rates 25bps to 5% at its August meeting, in a very close 5-4 vote. Bailey, Breeden and incoming member Lombardelli joined Ramsden and Dhingra in voting for a cut. The outcome was in line with our expectation and long held call for an August cut. There were mixed elements in the forward communications.

Even among the five voting for a cut, the decision was described as “finely balanced”. And Bailey at the press conference also spoke of stronger growth and still-high underlying inflation that meant “we need to be careful not to cut rates too much or too quickly”. We read this and the BoE’s forecasts (discussed more below) as signalling cuts at a pace of once per quarter from here, and that remains our view. We forecast the next cut in November.

That said, the BoE also judged that there is a fair degree of upside risk around its modal forecasts. Should that perception fade - for example if sequential services inflation slows more clearly or more slack opens up - then it would open the door to a faster easing cycle. But with growth having turned out strong lately, we don’t see this as a significant near-term risk, with the BoE remaining cautious.

The committee again batted away strong data and focussed on broader evidence of easing inflation persistence to justify a cut. While growth for 2024 was revised up from 0.4% to 1.2%, the underlying pace was characterized as softer. The Bank also produced a brand new measure of underlying services inflation that excludes a more comprehensive list of items that have been strong lately. This includes items that are indexed or regulated (e.g. telecom bills, vehicle excise duty, local authority rents) volatile components (airfares, hotels) rents and foreign holidays. Not surprisingly, services inflation has been better behaved on this basis.

This exclusion measure probably goes too far given the BoE does still see a significant risk of more persistent inflation and a potentially more permanent shift in wage and price setting (which will affect indexed items particularly, for example). But the BoE continues to place a significant weight on other survey based and forward looking measures of pricing (e.g. the DMP, inflation expectations) which it is taking more comfort from. Indeed, this is the main reason it concluded there has been “some progress in moderating risks of persistence in inflation”. Bailey stated that “we should expect that perceptions of current inflation and expectations of future inflation will play into wage bargaining and price setting”.

## Upside skew in the inflation forecast

Reflecting the close decision, the statement still spoke of a risk of ongoing persistence, and the forecasts show the reintroduction of upside skew to the BoE’s medium-term inflation projections. Their modal inflation forecast drops well below target to 1.7% in two years time, based on market rates. This is down from 1.9% previously and in isolation suggests rates could be cut more quickly than markets expect over the coming months. But this message is counterbalanced by the mean just about being being shown at 2.0% at the same horizon, albeit before also dropping back to 1.8%. This in contrast suggests the BoE will follow the market path more closely in the near term.

The BoE had presented a similar upside skew in its forecasts (Table 2) last year, before deciding that upside risks had disappeared in February. The fact they have been reintroduced is therefore noteworthy, especially as broader forward-looking inflation measures have moderated compared to last year. That may partly reflect still strong underlying inflation data. But the motivation this time round is more about the BoE experimenting with its communications and bringing in some early elements of the Bernanke review, i.e. shifting to a scenario-based analysis. But instead of doing so explicitly, it has done so using its existing forecasting framework.

This was made explicitly clear in the press conference, where Bailey spoke of three scenarios: 1) inflation being ultimately transitory as global shocks fade; 2) Inflation requiring a peri od of slack to meet the target; and 3) a permanent change in wage and price setting behavior, perhaps reflecting structural shifts, including a higher NAIRU. The modal forecast is supposed to capture the first two scenarios, while the mean captures an element of the third. The third was described by Bailey as a Bernanke-style “prototype economic scenario.” Bailey further commented that, “as we develop our response to Dr. Bernanke’s recommendations, we will be in a position to articulate fully such scenarios.”

In the past we were led to believe that the mean should be the main focus as an all encompassing assessment of the committee’s best collective judgement. This time it’s not quite as clear. Bailey said its approach could be considered scenario analysis, or simply as an upside risk to the forecast. Different members probably have different views. But our inclination is to focus more on the mean as the BoE’s risk adjusted collective view. That would imply it will follow a rate path close to market expectations - i.e. one more this year and a once per quarter pace in general. However, if those upside risks were judged to be fading, it could open up the door to faster cuts.

## Limited verbal forward guidance

Explicit forward guidance in the statement was quite limited with large parts removed. The MPC again spoke of policy needing to “remain restrictive for sufficiently long” but dropped the part saying that that rates should remain “restrictive for an extended period of time.” The BoE also removed the explicit focus on services inflation, wage growth and labour market conditions, perhaps because it has become frustrated with the excessive focus on particular data points and wants the freedom to consider the risks more broadly. Bailey echoed the point made by Lagarde and Powell lately, noting that while it pays close attention to services inflation, this does not mean that “we should adjust our course with every data surprise that comes in.” He spoke instead about the “accumulation of evidence about the medium-term outlook for inflation.”

The MPC’s statement now merely says that the MPC “continues to monitor closely the risks of inflation persistence and will decide the appropriate degree of monetary policy restrictiveness at each meeting.” Some have suggested this downplays the role of forecast meetings, but we don’t think the emphasis here has really shifted from before. On QT, the BoE has a box in the MPR reviewing use of the policy so far and highlighting a decision is due to September on the pace for the subsequent year. This gives little away in terms of what the MPC will vote for in terms of active sales. But the BoE continues to emphasize that sale will be conducted “in a relatively gradual and predictable manner.” Consistent with this, as well as Bailey’s preference to reduce interest risk on the BoE’s balance sheet, we find it hard to see how the MPC will vote to suddenly stop active sales.

## Emerging Europe

• Poland: Inflation jumps on regulated energy adjustments

• Hungary: GDP contracts in 2Q

• Czech Republic: muddles through

• Russia: Growth moderating, labor market still tight

## Poland: Inflation jumps

A five-month streak of CPI prints within the NBP’s target band (2.5%oya +/- 1%-pt) was interrupted by sharp adjustments to regulated energy prices in July. According to the preliminary CPI release, consumer prices increased in July by 1.4%m/m, resulting in a jump in the over-year-ago rate to 4.2%oya (from 2.6%), which is a bit lower than expected by markets (J.P. Morgan: 4.3%oya, Consensus: 4.4%).

The sharp monthly increase in CPI was expected, driven by 20%+ adjustments to regulated energy tariffs that were announced with plenty of notice, though the precise impact was uncertain. The “other energy” category (which includes the items subject to big increases, electricity and gas) was up 11.8%m/m, a bit less than we estimated (13.3%m/m) whereas fuel prices were flat (we expected +0.3%m/m) and food was roughly in line, declining 0.5%m/m (-0.6% expected).

Core CPI is not published with the flash release, but if we mechanically extract the contributions of non-core items (Table 1), we are left with a residual which suggests core prices were up 0.25-0.3%m/m, quite above our forecast (0.1%m/m). If confirmed, it implies core CPI ticking higher to 3.7%oya in May, from 3.6%, rather than falling marginally as we forecasted (3.5%), which would mark the first month of no decline after 15 consecutive prints of ever-lower readings. So looking at the last three months of data, core inflation is running at a momentum pace of approximately 4.25%.

1 July figures are preliminary 2 July core CPI is J.P. Morgan estimate

After this jump in energy prices, headline inflation is likely to run above core inflation for the next 12 months (Figure 1). There is some uncertainty at the start of 2025 as some energy support measures expire and could result in another jump in

CPI. We expect however that the Government will extend such measures: with the Climate minister supportive for that option, but ultimately the call is from the Ministry of Finance who has not yet given final guidance. On our assumptions, headline will end 2025 at the top of the NBP’s target band (2.5%oya +/- 1%-pt). Core inflation's trend descent is over, with momentum now running above the over-year-ago rate, But in August specifically it is likely to fall again to sub 3.5% due to a favourable base effect.

With the NBP adopting a hawkish posture, against a background of cuts everywhere, we expect that the Zloty will be well supported and core inflation momentum can actually decline. We forecast core stuck in the 3.5-4% range until end-2025, but the more hawkish the NBP remains the more risks can build on the downside. We expect the NBP will cut only in 2Q25, but even there our conviction isn't high, it could well start in 1Q or later in 3Q for example. Regardless we expect only be a shallow easing, with the central bank bringing rates to 5% at end-2025.

## CEE: 2Q GDP disappoints

Regional growth remains subdued in the CEE region, with the consumer’s recovery modest relative to expectations (on the back of large real wage gains), and insufficient to fully compensate for headwinds elsewhere, in particular in export-oriented industry. The two available flash GDP releases (Czech Republic and Hungary) show downside surprises (of different magnitudes though) versus both our expectations and the market’s. We had anticipated growth of 0.5% in each country, which turned out to be a bit optimistic in the Czech case, where GDP was up 0.3% on the quarter, but in Hungary the economy contracted by 0.2%, a big disappointment. When put in context of rather weak growth at European level, and in Germany in particular, Czech growth does not appear that bad, being actually one of the top reading among the countries who published 2Q data thus far, but in Hungary’s case, the data is outright bad.

Figure 2: Czech Real GDP growth %q/q saar
![](images/db71b643347ada5ff71505aa171e4d17030c86d4d22b33f4ca9ee2925504dcc4.jpg)

Figure 3: Hungary Real GDP growth %q/q saar
![](images/f5cda72287c7aa8b61ce0aae9c9ee811bef6e9694ef8ab6e0dab074a6dae98e2.jpg)

We lack for now any breakdown in both releases, but there respective statistical office’s press release disclose that for Czech growth was supported by domestic demand, particular ly consumption while external demand had a negative impacts. For Hungary real estate activity as well as the balance of taxes and subsidies was supportive while the main drag from industry.The weakness in these prints combined with, broad-based growth weakness in the region’s industryintensive countries (Germany, Austria, Hungary) curbs expectations over the potential for acceleration near-term. We are tweaking our our Czech growth forecasts lower. For Czech our full year growth expectations to 1%, down from 1.2%, then picking up to 2% in 2025. For Hungary We now see growth of just 1.4% in 2024, to then accelerate to 2.3% in 2025.

Central banks should be reassured by the lack of demand side pressure. Inflation data will ultimately dominate and persistence price pressure can co exits for some time in the a context of tight labour markets, staggered wage bargaining, pandemic-shocked inflation expectations and in specific cases, Government stimulus or decisions on large minimum wage increases. The CNB has shifted the pace of rate cuts to 25bps this week, regardless of the GDP data but weak activity could (if CPI data continues to behave benignly) open the door for rates to fall below 4% at the end of the year. In the case of Hungary the inflationary problem appears more serious, with core CPI surprising to the upside in the last four prints. We expect the NBH will cut two more times by 25bps this year

(to 6.25% at year end), with the bar to cut more relatively high due to the high level of underlying inflation.

## Czech Republic: CNB starts fine-tuning

The CNB cut its 2-week repo rate by 25bps to 4.5%, slowing the pace of easing from previous months, all as expected. The decision passed with the unanimous support of the board (7-0), which was not a given but was highly likely given the nature of the central bank’s most recent public commentary.

The research department published new macro projections, tweaking growth expectations marginally, down in 2024 to 1.2% (from 1.4%) and up in 2025 to 2.8% (from 2.7%). The inflation path is barely changed, slight lower in 2H24 and then marginally higher in late 2025. Once again the path of EUR/CZK is lifted, in part also as external assumptions for Euribor are lifted making for a narrower gap vis-a-vis Czech rates. Finally, the rate projection is also lifted 15bps at end-2024 (to 4.43%) and 45bps at end-2025 (to 3.59) if this is taken at face value, this would imply no more rate cuts this year.

The statement remains extremely hawkish-leaning, despite disappointing activity figure and the better reading on May and June inflation. The board assesses risks to inflation to be to the upside with every cut to be considered with great caution. The threat is reiterated: the “can be paused or terminated at any time at levels that are still restrictive.”

Everything suggests the board now has less confidence about further easing, and hence will move in small steps of 25bps, but clearly there is not much enthusiasm for much more at least if the currency continues to behave on the weak side. We continue to expect two additional rate cuts this year, in September and November, bringing rates to 4%. Behind this call, we assume inflation will be above target on trend, even if sub 3%. If inflation were to trend at target, we think the CNB would cut a bit more then our base case, by another 25-50bps.

## Russia: Growth slows, labor market tight

The distribution of growth surprises in Russia has become more even — a sign that growth is finally moderating. In June, hard activity data surprised significantly to the downside, with both supply and demand-side indicators coming on the soft side. We lowered our tracking of 2Q growth to below 1%q/q, saar. Given the carryover from a weak June, as well as the anticipated effects of policy tightening and of lower agricultural output this year, we think that 3Q GDP will not grow much above zero.

Although business surveys are downshifting, they still signal a slightly above-potential growth in the current quarter. More structural growth drivers — higher fiscal spending, import

substitution, high investment to rebuild supply chains — remain intact for now, That may suggest that beyond cyclical weakness the underlying growth may remain solid. The sector that has remained red-hot despite the recent downshift in growth is the labor market — the unemployment rate slid further down to 2.5%, sa, in June, with nominal wage growth sticky in high teens. And this tightness is likely to outweigh any concerns about growth slowdown on the part of the CBR, at least in the near term, given the unpleasant implications of a tight labor market for the inflation outlook.

We expect that inflation in Hungary picked up to 4.0%oya in July (from 3.7%), driven by most subcomponents on the back of a less favorable base. Food prices likely rose to 0.3%oya from -1.8%, and there is considerable uncertainty as the Government stopped enforcing a scheme of mandatory discount on food retailers, so risks are skewed to the upside. Fuel prices likely also picked up to 5%oya from 3.2%. Turning to core we expect that core inflation accelerated to 4.6%oya from 4.1%, mainly on the back of a rise in processed food. Excluding all food, the core measure should tick marginally lower to 5.5%oya from 5.6%.

Source: NBH, Local sources, J.P. Morgan Forecasts

## South Africa

• June fiscal data broadly on track for full-year 4.3% target

• However, corporate tax receipts are soft, underscoring moderate downside risk

We expect a 4.6% deficit with a further tick higher in the debt ratio

Data for the first quarter of the fiscal year suggest that the fiscal balance is largely on track to reach the 4.3% deficit target for FY24/25, after a 4.6% outcome in FY23/24, although with risks of 0.2%pts-0.3%pts of GDP in slippage. June receipts benefit from strong seasonality from corporates with the balance recording a R38.6bn surplus, marginally up on the R36.8bn in June 2023. This brought the fiscal deficit for the first three months of the fiscal year to 0.7% of GDP, on par with the performance in FY23/24, with a 12 months trailing deficit improving marginally further to 4.6% of GDP (Figure 1).

Figure 1: Revenue ratio and rolling fiscal deficit
![](images/fc0257ddbbfa9da30467d9059a81324b10cface3c457f5eaea35db5679274a0d.jpg)
Source: National Treasury, J.P.Morgan calculations

Looking at the detail underscores our view of measured downside risk to revenue targets, particularly for corporate tax receipts. Government revenue growth has eased to 2.7%oya in 2Q24 (-0.9%oya in June) as corporate tax receipts contracted 6.6%oya (-6.9%oya in June), likely on lesser contribution from the mining corporates. Consequently, government revenue in 2Q24 amounted to just 23% of the full-year target, against 23.6% in 2Q23.

For now, spending restraint sufficiently mitigates softer revenue momentum as expenditure growth was contained to 3.7%oya last quarter. Moreover, receipts should increasingly reflect the uplift from bracket creep to personal tax receipts in coming months, compensating softer corporate receipts. Even so, risks are likely skewed towards a small underperformance in tax receipts this year. Consequently, we retain our 4.6% main budget deficit projection (Treasury: -4.3%). In our view, this probably supports an upward drift in the government debt ratio this year.

## Australia and New Zealand

• Australian 2Q headline CPI was in line with expectations, trimmed mean surprised to the downside

• This should see the RBA on hold next week

• And we still do not expect a first cut until February 2025

We anticipate the NZ unemployment rate rose 0.3%-pt in 2Q24 to 4.6%

Australia’s 2Q inflation report headlined a bumper week of data in the Antipodes. The CPI data were not as strong as expected, with trimmed mean (core) inflation at 0.8%q/q, below our already sub-consensus forecast of 0.9%q/q (Bloomberg consensus: 1.0%q/q). Headline was in line with our/consensus estimate at 1.0%q/q. The annual rates remain in the high 3s, which for headline marked a slight acceleration as expected to 3.8%oya, while trimmed mean eased slightly to 3.9%oya. Two of the last three trimmed mean results have annualized near the top of the RBA’s 2-3% target band, at 3.2%.

The RBA has 2H24 forecast downgrades up its sleeve for the August SoMP, thanks to the Budget’s subsidy. Even assuming payback when these are let go in the 2H25 point of the forecast path, near-term subsidy cover for the remainder of this year, along with likely moderation in other groups should be enough for the RBA to remain on hold in August.

Australia Data releases and forecasts Week of Aug 5 - 9
Figure 1: Australian CPI basket distribution
Proportion of CPI basket annualizing above given threshold (sa)
![](images/a43185d628c3f6f913d8590fd77e045ba489e15610acbb0cdcf69ff07000260b.jpg)

We wrote here that the return of core inflation to the top of the target band is closer than it appears, given that the proportion of subgroups posting strong inflation outcomes has thinned significantly, and is in areas which generally tend to lag rather than lead inflation. That continued in 2Q with the right tail of CPI items (proportion growing above 2%q/q saar, above 3% and 4%) moderating further (Figure 1). The proportion of items annualizing in the 2-3% target band is now normal.

The remaining pesky, sticky groups are alcohol and tobacco, and parts of health, education and insurance, which are either likely to ease following lagged headline/indexation factors, or face administered/regulated price constraints. Of course, the absence of right tail items also leaves less scope for very sharp moves lower in headline from here which would put inflation sustainably inside the target band on an %oya basis in the next few quarters.

## Retail up and down

Though CPI was broadly weaker than anticipated, Wednesday’s retail data were mixed. On the positive side, increased 0.5%m/m in June, ahead of both J.P. Morgan/consensus forecasts (+0.2%m/m) and the second consecutive monthly upside surprise. However, the 2Q volumes release was less upbeat with turnover slipping 0.3%q/q, indicating that the strength in the monthly prints has been mostly a price story.

Retail volumes have now declined in seven of the past eight quarters (Figure 2) and remain 2% below the turnover level when the RBA first hiked in May 2022. We expect private consumption as measured in GDP to outperform the retail data given its greater skew to the services sector, but this week’s data is still a reminder that the household sector remains fragile. That said, our baseline is for consumption to gradually improve from current levels as the combination of mid-year tax cuts, rising real wage growth and fading monetary policy drags (we expect the RBA to stay on hold this year with easing likely from early 2025) to support spending.

Figure 2: Australian retail volumes
![](images/62ab4f252eda5ed1fa280b6da0e0cf2256a8bf516e2823d7438c8ec225718ff8.jpg)

<table><tr><td>Tue</td><td>RBA official cash rate decision</td></tr><tr><td>Aug 6</td><td>Mar</td><td>May</td><td>Jun</td><td>Aug</td></tr><tr><td>2:30pm</td><td>%</td><td>4.35</td><td>4.35</td><td>4.35 4.35</td></tr></table>

With 2Q CPI out of the way, attention turns to the RBA meeting next week. We expect the Bank to remain on hold, while continuing to sound vigilant about risks of more persistent

inflation. Unlike other central banks, the leadership hasn’t entertained the prospect of easing, and that message should be maintained in the statement guidance “it will be some time yet before inflation is sustainably in the target range”. In the press conference, Governor Bullock will likely deflect questions about cuts.

That messaging is appropriate as the leadership has balanced their objectives by pursuing a gradual return to the inflation target. The compromise on the way up - applying a flexible timeline to an already flexible inflation objective (a band) - is that they can’t act as pre-emptively on the way down. We have expected the RBA to be on hold this year with a first cut in February 2025, at the rear of the pack of DM central banks

The Bank will also provide an updated forecast set next week We expect the staff’s new Dec-24 projection for headline will be 3.3%oya, near where it was in Feb, and a 50bp reduction vs. the prior forecasts. Trimmed mean should be the same, which means both headline and core fall roughly 0.5%-pt over the second half of the year. Looking further out, the prior forecasts had already implied trimmed mean would drop inside the target band at Dec-2025. That should now come forward to Jun-25 (2.8%). The staff’s unemployment forecasts are largely tracking, while growth needs to be marked higher to reflect ABS revisions in 1Q, but don’t otherwise look off track from a forward-looking, sequential perspective.

Turning to NZ, the labor market has been weakening since late 2022, and recent data suggest a break into more pronounced slowdown. The NZIER business survey for 2Q was bleak and registered the lowest reading on numbers newly employed in the quarter since the GFC. This has since been backed up in NZ Stats’ monthly labor market series, in which filled jobs contracted in the June quarter.

In the June quarter report we look for employment growth of 0.2%q/q, and a 0.3%-pt rise in the unemployment rate to 4.6%, the highest level since mid-2018. Job advertisements suggest a much steeper rise in the unemployment rate is possible, though we are wary of the fact that the vacancy unwind has been in train for a while and is a global phenomenon, with excess labor demand moderating most in the inventory of unfilled positions.

With the economy still operating well below potential, further deterioration in the labor market is likely, and in our view makes the RBNZ likely to start easing at the next meeting in August.

## Greater China

China: July Politburo’s mid-year assessment gives higher priority to boosting consumption

• July manufacturing PMIs point to cautious outlook on near-term activity

• Hong Kong: 2Q GDP beat expectations at 3.3%oya growth; revising up full-year growth forecast to 3.0%

Taiwan: 2Q GDP rose 5.1%oya with easing consumption, capex bounce and a net export drag

• July manufacturing PMI stayed elevated; consumer sentiment strengthened further

• Next week: China trade, FX reserves, inflation and credit, Taiwan CPI and trade

After the 3<sup>rd</sup> Plenum meeting laid out the medium-term reform agenda, the July Politburo meeting has been closely watched for near-term policy guidance. The statement assessed that “overall economic development was stable” and pointed out the challenges faced by the Chinese economy, such as changes in the external environment, “insufficient domestic demand”, “divergent performance in economic activity”, “risks in key areas”, and “pains in the transition from old to new growth drivers”. It seems that policymakers are concerned, but do not feel the urgency for a meaningful policy shift in 2H.

Comparing the 2H policy guidance with the April politburo meeting statement, we note two changes. First, boosting consumption received high priority in the 2H policy guidance (though not completely new). In particular, it took service consumption as an important area to expand and upgrade (e.g. cultural tourism, elderly care, childcare and housekeeping). This raised the hope that, after the recent announcement of consumer goods trade-in subsidies (in auto, home appliance), the government may also roll out new measures to support service consumption.

Second, the statement raised the need to address problems in policy implementation and market disorder. When the Politburo continued to call for promoting new-quality productive forces, it added “to strengthen industry self-discipline and prevent ‘involutionary’ vicious competition”. This is relevant to the criticism of excess capacity and excess competition in the Chinese economy, contributing to problems such as lack of pricing power for corporates, deflation pressure and escalation in trade tensions.

Nonetheless, the 2H policy guidelines are mostly similar to the policy guidance from the April politburo meeting. It reinforces our view that while macro policy is increasingly growth friendly, the probability of an additional stimulus

package is low. On the fiscal side, the focus is to fully utilize the fiscal space (e.g. expanded scope of use of special Treasury bond, and likely expanded scope of use of special local government bond). On the monetary policy front, the policy language of “use rate and RRR instruments” in the April politburo meeting statement disappeared, suggesting that the PBOC’s monetary easing remains cautious after July’s rate cuts (our forecasts expect no further rate cut, one 25bp RRR cut, and sluggish credit growth for the rest of the year). A possible surprise may come from the statement that “(macro policy) should reserve and ready to launch a batch of incremental policy measures in a timely manner”.

## Industrial profits grew 3.6%oya

China’s industrial profits rose 3.6%oya in June. By sector breakdown, we estimate the share of mainstream raw material and machinery & equipment manufacturing sectors in total industrial profits rose to 43.0% in Jan-Jun (vs. 40.7% in Jan-May) (Figure 1). This is attributable to the policy support on industrial upgrade and innovation from the domestic front, and solid demand from external front.

Figure 1: China: Industrial enterprise profits
![](images/790930284282a3f22e4c1894e2e472eaa1ae96b339f13dc1557b1beb7371ea57.jpg)
Source: NBS, J.P. Morgan. Note: EHGW = Electricity, Heat, Gas, Water Production & Supply

The growth rate of finished goods inventory has been grinding at the bottom of the current cycle, only picking up recently. Over-year-ago growth remained in low single-digits since 2H23, and registered at 4.7%oya in June. But this does not indicate a low inventory level. In fact, industrial enterprise finished product inventory level has been above its trend since 2022. Meanwhile, the inventory to sales ratio, which better captures the dynamics of supply and demand, has been on the rise since 2021, though it retreated somewhat in 2H23. The unwinding co-movement of inventory growth and inventory to sales ratio since 2023 reflects weak domestic demand conditions in China.

## July PMIs point to cautious outlook

Following the downside surprise in 2Q GDP, with June activity data highlighting a fragile consumption trend, the July NBS manufacturing PMI edged down 0.1-pt to 49.4, and the non-manufacturing PMI fell 0.3-pt to 50.2. Meanwhile, the Caixin manufacturing PMI fell 2.0-pt to 49.8, reversing five consecutive monthly gains through June, with the July reading registering at the lowest level since October last year. The 4.4-pt plunge in the Caixin output component (to 49.2) hints at a slowing in near-term export-related industrial production activity.

Recall that the Caixin manufacturing PMI has generally outperformed the NBS manufacturing PMI since 2Q23, consistent with solid performance of the export sector (net exports accounted for 0.7%-pt of the 5.0%oya headline GDP growth in 1H24). The 2.0-pt decline in the July Caixin manufacturing PMI to 49.8 seems to have closed the gap with the NBS manufacturing PMI (at 49.4 in July) to a large extent (Figure 2).

Figure 2: China Manufacturing PMIs
![](images/e02c84b6b1564fed8eb49c61be0c3566315709d5e81b174fdc0c229a757dc2c9.jpg)
Source: S&P Global, NBS, J.P. Morgan

The Caixin manufacturing PMI input price component fell 1.8-pt to 50.5 in July, while the output price component fell 1.2-pt to 49.8. This seems to reflect the impact of insufficient demand conditions, sending cautious signals about producer pricing power, and may hint at lingering pressure for Chinese exporters’ price competition in the global market.

Putting together the combined signals from the July NBS PMIs and Caixin manufacturing PMI, it appears China’s economic momentum remains on the soft side in the near term. Lingering softness in service and consumption is the weak link for the economy, while the export sector, a crucial driver of growth in recent quarters, may have started to lose some momentum. This highlights the crucial role of macro policy to stabilize growth going into 2H.

## Hong Kong: 2Q GDP grew 3.3%oya

Hong Kong’s 2Q24 GDP (advanced estimate) beat expectations, rising 3.3%oya in real terms. Seasonally adjusted, 2Q

GDP rose 1.6%q/q saar, adding to the 10.4% rise in 1Q (Figure 3). In the breakdown, real private consumption expenditure (PCE) fell 1.6%oya, the first over-year-ago contraction since 3Q22. Government consumption expenditure rose 2.0%oya in 2Q24, compared with the 2.2%oya decline in 1Q24. Goods exports and imports continued to grow, rising 7.6%oya and 3.4%oya, respectively. Exports of services slowed, rising 1.3%oya in 2Q24, down from 9.4%oya in 1Q24 and 21.2%oya in 4Q23. Imports of services remained solid, rising 12.4%oya in 2Q24. Gross domestic fixed capital formation (GFCF) rose 6.0%oya, compared with +0.1%oya in 1Q.

Figure 3: Hong Kong real GDP growth
![](images/8544a5f78639c822f6d7f10234dbd5c7360ba2bc1e66dfef8930343a8e916352.jpg)

By contribution, we estimate that the 3.3%oya growth was mainly lifted by net exports (+3.7%pt), while investment contributed 0.5%pt, and consumption dragged by 0.9%pt. Note that this is the first quarter in which consumption made a negative contribution to over-year-ago real GDP growth since 3Q22. Following the upside surprise in 2Q24 GDP data, we revise up our 2024 full-year growth forecast to 3.0%y/y (previously 2.8%y/y), and expect full-year growth to be supported by net exports.

## Retail sales improved marginally

Hong Kong’s retail sales volume came in above expectations in June, falling 11.2%oya. Seasonally adjusted, we estimate that retail sales volume rose 1.4%m/m sa, reversing the 1.2%m/m sa loss in May. This is the first m/m sa gain since February, possibly reflecting some signs of stabilization in retail activities. For 2Q as a whole, retail sales volume fell 13.5%oya, or down 21.4%q/q saar by our estimate.

Looking ahead, we believe inbound tourism will still be the driving factor of consumption growth in HK. In July, visitor arrivals from the Mainland averaged at 77.2% of the pre-pandemic level (vs. 70.4% in June), while outbound HK residents averaged at 114.1% of the pre-pandemic level (vs. 120.5% in June). July and August used to record seasonal-high level for inbound tourism, often related with Mainland student summer vacation and incoming freshman year college students. Meanwhile, the low unemployment environment, household

income growth, together with various promotional campaigns and activities launched by the government, should continue to support retail growth. Overall, we expect some sequential improvement in retail sales in the near team.

## Taiwan: 2Q GDP rose 5.1%oya

Taiwan’s advanced 2Q GDP came in above consensus and below our expectations, rising 5.1%oya (Figure 4). It implies marginal sequential growth of 0.13% q/q saar, following 1.1% growth in 1Q (Figure 5). The disappointment in 2Q GDP, compared to our forecast, is partly related to consumer spending, which slowed to 1.0% q/q saar growth in 2Q (despite a notable uptick in sequential growth trend of retail sales). Exports of goods and services eased modestly (-0.5% q/q saar) in 2Q, consistent with the monthly export data trend. Meanwhile, imports of goods and services showed significant expansion (28.5% q/q saar), outpacing the growth pace suggested by monthly import data and exerting a notable drag on headline GDP.

Figure 4: Taiwan real GDP growth % change, both scales
![](images/2ee5df3afd6888112808824ea174f8a7280be822460b04798b894f1861ff941f.jpg)
Source: DGBAS, J.P. Morgan

Following a notable contraction during the tech down cycle (3Q22-3Q23), the corporate sector appears to have resumed capex spending significantly, as gross capital formation surged 67.1% q/q saar in 2Q. Going forward, we look for a steady up-trend in Taiwan’s tech exports going into 2H. The latest upturn in Taiwan’s domestic capex trend is encouraging, likely reflecting Taiwan manufacturers’ constructive outlook on external demand conditions for the coming quarters. Solid external sector activity appears to be feeding to the domestic front, leading to a steady uptick in employment growth (and hence household income), which could help to support domestic demand growth going into 2H.

We expect Taiwan’s economy to show steady sequential growth ahead, with average 2.6% q/q saar in 2H. As 2Q GDP came in below our expectation, our forecast for full-year 2024 growth has been revised down to 4.2%yoy.

## July PMI stayed elevated

Following the volatile, moderate upward trend (from subdued levels) since early 2023, Taiwan’s manufacturing PMI readings have picked up momentum significantly since early this year. The latest signals confirmed a solid reading in July headline manufacturing PMI, which edged down 0.3-pt to a still elevated level of 52.9 (Figure 5). While the output component fell 1.0-pt in July, the 53.9 reading is still the second highest since August 2021, continuing to point to solid near-term momentum in industrial production activity. Regarding external demand conditions, signals from the manufacturing PMI export orders component remain quite constructive,with a modest decline of 0.2-pt to 52.2, hinting at a constructive near-term outlook for external demand.

Figure 5: Global and Taiwan manufacturing PMIs
![](images/05216725843db171c1424eec4b3e41d6dfcf429494e486f1b211f65404da542c.jpg)

Regarding supply chain conditions, it is worth noting that the suppliers’ delivery times reading fell 3.0-pt to 44.4 in July, adding to the decline of 1.6-pt in June. It registered the lowest reading since May 2022, suggesting a lengthening in suppliers’ delivering time. On the domestic front, Taiwan consumer confidence in the economic outlook has ticked up in the three months through July, along with a solid pick up in non-manufacturing PMI, confirming improving household confidence.

## Korea

• Tech production rises as inventory fall in June

• Tech exports took a breather in July, yet PMI signal that growth momentum remains intact

## • CPI inflation firmed up in July with core price gains

This week’s manufacturing sector data suggest an ongoing tech sector growth impulse. Tech production rose strongly in June with a surge in shipments and a sharp correction in inventories, and the manufacturing PMI’s new exports order held up at an elevated level. While July tech product exports fell likely with a technical payback, the overall view on the manufacturing sector cycle remain upbeat for 3Q. Meanwhile, the CPI inflation firmed up in July with gains in core prices, and the Bank of Korea is likely to remain cautious on the disinflation path going forward. We continue to expect a cautious approach by the BoK to normalize the policy rate, starting from 4Q24.

## Tech production rises as inventory fall

Industrial production modestly rose 0.5%m/m, sa, in June as we expected after a 0.6% fall in May, and the three-month trend growth recovered to 4.7%3m/3m, saar, from -1.2%3m/3m, saar, in March, which was also in line with our expectation for a quarterly IP growth. June’s production gain was led by a strong rise in tech production, which was accompanied by a sharp rise in shipments and a fall in inventory (Figure 1).

Figure 1: Tech production and inventory
![](images/3b5543d018c2ba66dee3d6921fbd14ffec4822e3a273aabdb6aa1378d9b73175.jpg)

In the sector details, tech production rose 5.5%m/m, sa, for the second monthly gain, as semiconductor production rose 8.1% following a 1.5% gain in May. Non-tech production fell 1.3% further following 1.4% fall in May, likely as a payback after a 5.0% rise in April. Looking forward, we expect a rotation of a short-term IP growth driver from non-tech to tech in 3Q as the details of tech shipments and inventory continue to be encouraging in June. That is, tech product shipments rose strongly by 17.9%m/m, sa, for the second monthly gain, and tech inventory fell 13.0% for the second monthly drop. As a result, the tech inventory to shipment ratio fell to 92.1, which is the lowest level since August 2021 (Figure 1), suggesting strong production growth going forward.

Figure 2: Korea manufacturing output - PMI vs. IP
![](images/a6a118bead123098fd8a2316a15357c32430ef71f1534a99fb0a40646e8ab8a3.jpg)

To be sure, manufacturing PMI fell 0.6pt in July yet the PMI level is still above the post-pandemic average, and the gradual recovery trend since 4Q22 remains intact. The current PMI output index suggests an upside bias to the IP’s trend growth (Figure 2).

## Customs exports took a breather in July

Customs exports fell 1.7%m/m, sa in July after a 1.8% gain in June, and the three-month trend growth moderated to 8.2%3m/3m, saar from 12.9% in June. Tech product exports took a breather, falling 4.6%m/m, sa after rising strongly for three months - the three-month trend growth of tech exports remains strong at 46.4%3m/3m, saar. Non-tech exports edged down 0.3%m/m, sa for the third monthly fall with mixed details. While the customs exports undershot expectations with tech exports’ correction, we expect that the underlying momentum remains intact for the tech sector exports growth, as hinted by manufacturing PMI details including the elevated exports order index (Figure 3).

Figure 3: Korea export orders PMI vs. customs exports
![](images/c3aff30e3d802dbb971f54f3dd5edb71b54adfeeace18275059ce636c31e26ee.jpg)

## CPI inflation firmed in July

The consumer price index rose 2.6%oya in July. This was a firmer print than our expectation, after the deceleration to 2.4% by June. Seasonally adjusted by J.P. Morgan, the headline CPI rose 0.3%m/m, sa, and core (ex. energy and food) rose 0.2%. In an alternative core measure (ex. oil and agriculture), prices rose a bit firmer at 0.3%m/m, sa. On our seasonal adjustment for detailed indices, July’s firm CPI print was mainly due to core goods and service prices’ monthly gains, as non-core prices (oil and fresh farm product prices) were not particularly strong. Strong monthly gains came in non-oil industrial goods prices (+0.3%m/m, sa), personal services (+0.3%) and dining out services (+0.2%).

Looking ahead, headline CPI inflation is still likely to gradually moderate through the rest of 2024, and there is a downward bias in August’s over-year-ago inflation due to a high base last year. Over-year-ago inflation should hover around 2.0-2.3% by the end of this year. On policy, we do not think the July print will materially change the Bank of Korea’s inflation forecast path, and it has acknowledged that progress on the ‘last mile’ of the disinflation path can be bumpy. We continue to expect the BoK to cut the base policy rate by 25bp in 4Q24.

## ASEAN

Thailand’s factory output disappoints in June; uneven and tepid recovery on the cards

Domestic demand remains stable with wide sectoral differences

Current account posts strong beat; outlook still constructive with rising core trade surplus as upside risk

Indonesia’s inflation eases in June; external conditions allow for 25bp rate cuts in 4Q24 and 1Q25

Thailand’s manufacturing production index (MPI) fell 1.7%oya in June (May: -1.5%oya), posting another downside surprise (JPM: -1.2%oya; consensus: -1.2%oya). On a sequential basis, factory output contracted 1.0%m/m, sa, although the underlying trend improved to 15.0%3m/3m, saar (May: 6.4%3m/3m, saar). The key drags came from cyclical, export-led categories (e.g., auto, electronics and machinery; Figure 1), while domestic-oriented industries (e.g., food, refined petroleum, base metals) fared better.

Figure 1: Thailand manufacturing production index (MPI) Dec19=100, sa
![](images/0bd607bd9679d695e16c597c7f048e5c33f34de3e8662a51b5672b4b7c81a0a4.jpg)

Figure 2: Thailand manufacturing PMI
![](images/205af7530bbc24aedf58a6e01ed778c7fb0b2d3861cf880727f4ecb08e4b5aab.jpg)

Weakness is consistent with the latest PMI showing a sharp fall in new export orders vis-à-vis overall new orders (Figure 2). Despite a healthier inventory profile (Figure 3), manufacturing recovery will likely be uneven and tepid, given structural issues affecting the auto and electronics industries.

Figure 3: Thailand manufacturing PMI Diffusion index
![](images/243764c9ef47432db548fc4be312bceafff2fced118ba7c7df326725ed3aeaab.jpg)
Source: S&P Global, J.P. Morgan

The private consumption indicator (PCI) remained unchanged (-0.2%m/m, sa; 1.2%oya) in June, with weakness in durables offset by a pick-up in non-durables and non-resident spending. The private investment indicator (PII) rose slightly (1.4%m/m, sa; 1.3%oya) and showed sharp sectoral divergences as well, with construction material and domestic machinery sales vastly outperforming capital goods imports and registered motor vehicle sales.

Figure 4: Thailand domestic demand indicators
![](images/a60189b1c06a18812c7a58db6bbd3381b9cd68bd3d6d23fc7d6965f70d08cdb9.jpg)

Figure 5: Thailand private consumption indicator (PCI)
![](images/289fa5720033eda3ce0f282b7e063a423a50e63547a397c9620b21e73549d711.jpg)

Through the noisy monthly data underlying momentum has remained steady, since cooling after the initial reopening surge (Figure 4). This is driven by the normalization of tourism incomes and fiscal spending, albeit with sectoral differences (Figure 5 and 6). The sharp fall in durables spending is to some extent policy-induced, as the Bank of Thailand (BoT) pursues an integrated approach (e.g., policy rate, macro-prudential measures) to achieve household debt deleveraging.

Source: BPS

Figure 6: Private investment indicator (PII) components 4Q19=100, sa
![](images/4898a1b462efd8ce41897717bf13cae9abd077612fe9ecdbd6a43abdbd78b0d1.jpg)

The current account (CA) rose to a surplus of US\$2.0 billion in June (May: US\$0.6 billion), coming in significantly above expectations (JPM: -US\$0.2 billion; consensus: US\$0.4 billion). The CA improvement was driven by stronger goods and invisible balances. We maintain a constructive view of the CA balance (2024F: 1.2% of GDP), with the recent increase in the non-energy goods surplus (Figure 7) posing an upside risk to our estimate.

Figure 7: Thailand BOP goods balance
![](images/07580713ff41fac94f39446a3c02122df8a72e21d3e73ec5ed4d16beecedeb88.jpg)

## Indonesia: July CPI moderates further

Indonesia’s July headline CPI came in weaker than expected, up 2.1%oya (JPM: 2.3%oya; consensus: 2.4%oya) and down 0.1%m/m, sa. Core inflation came in at 2.0%oya and was up 0.1%m/m, sa. The underlying trend came down for both headline and core inflation (Figure 8). Softness in food prices (-0.27%pt. contribution to monthly headline change; Figure 9), particularly essential food items (meat, shallot, etc.) continue to pull headline inflation down. Underlying core good and services prices have picked up (Figure 10), driven by household maintenance, recreation and personal care, but remain below pre-pandemic run-rates.

Figure 8: Indonesia consumer prices
![](images/f6f496adb3d1748363a5e3612d3d6c2e284f4ac9f4366f8748ff4cf3d0c0a1c4.jpg)

Figure 9: Indonesia: sources of inflation
![](images/bdc916d50a45578b4b03500f964a65c28e267e116718a479f1eb7458879b8c72.jpg)

Figure 10: Indonesia core CPI %3m/3m, saar
![](images/016a97575756751be1762385a33cfc7193dedbdd8bf43c4ee521032395325582.jpg)

All things considered, the trajectory of inflation is broadly in line with our expectations. We continue to expect headline CPI to average around mid point of 2024 inflation target (1.5-3.5%oya) at 2.4%oya (Figure 11), penciling in direct and indirect fiscal support on top of the already impressive set of supply-side measures implemented over the past several years.

Data releases and forecasts

Week of Aug 05 - Aug 09

% change

Week of Aug 05 - Aug 09
Figure 11: Indonesia headline CPI
![](images/583396f48d121a9374d0d0570476e71484999088d7ccf687ffbac19857525ada.jpg)
In addition to price stability, Bank Indonesia’s (BI) reaction function is highly sensitive to external condition and the broad US dollar, and given the increasing narrative around possible cut by Fed in Sept, we continue to expect BI to deliver 25bp policy cuts in 4Q24 and 1Q25.

## India

RBI is expected to remain on hold and retain it’s stance at next week’s monetary policy review

• Meanwhile, high frequency data in recent months have been a mixed bag

Bank credit growth, GST collections and corporate earnings are all gradually slowing

But auto sales rebounded in July from a soft May and June

The RBI is expected to remain comfortably on hold at next week’s monetary policy review, and reiterate its “withdrawal of accommodation” stance. While the outlook on food prices is slowly getting better as the monsoon improves, the central bank will need to see more evidence that food inflation is softening durably and thereby pushing headline CPI closer to the 4% inflation target. The RBI will also be mindful of financial stability concerns given the surge in unsecured lending to households over the last 18 months, though those dynamics are progressively softening as we show below. The RBI has already previously tightened macro prudential measures, but will worry that any easing of monetary conditions could fuel more risk-taking behavior. For all these reasons, we expect the RBI to remain firmly on hold for now, even as we expect some easing towards the end of the year in response to a softening of growth momentum and once there is more conviction that inflation is settling below 5%.

## Credit growth slows, mix healthier

Bank credit growth has been gradually softening in recent months (Figure 1). The softening has been led largely by a slowing of unsecured lending to households and lending to NBFCs. Recall, the RBI had imposed several macro prudential measures last year to slow the surge in unsecured lending and these measures appear to be bearing fruit.

Figure 1: Bank Credit growth
![](images/a79e8f59c4ce3dfbe0aed51c16fce597389f7af65a40826b00a4d7e078e56ac6.jpg)
Secured lending to households has been picking up reflecting a pick-up in mortgages, and continued momentum in the housing sector, so the mix of lending to households is becom-

ing healthier (Figure 2). Loan growth to commercial real estate has also picked up smartly in recent months. Deposit growth has not been able to keep up with credit growth of late, suggesting the latter may be becoming a binding constraint for new lending.

Figure 2: Secured vs unsecured personal loans
![](images/2362e4ba4712fd63f73834ba42def0c7fdb56bd5f23839952962b6a3d01c67e1.jpg)

## High frequency data are a mixed bag

An early read of corporate earnings for 2Q suggests a slowdown in EBITDA (earnings before interest, taxes and depreciation) growth. A sample of about 800 non-financial private corporations reports that EBITDA growth slowed from 11% in 1Q to 9% in 2Q. However top-line growth has remained broadly unchanged at 7% oya. Additionally, a sample of about 400 manufacturing firms reveals a further slowdown from a lackluster 1Q performance (Figure 3).

Figure 3: EBDITA of private manufacturing corporates
![](images/faa4ce295cd8616d40839fc0ab2d0ad3ace1195b9d9ba3ee3a68ad0a2536e78e.jpg)

Figure 4: GST Revenue (Nominal)
![](images/cd36a91eb173340279bd80a62eea3421a6d5168c9dbf3c7819768ec93ad25f2f.jpg)

Finally, GST collections in July rebounded to grow 10.3% from 7.7% the previous month. But, as Figure 4 reveals, GST collections have gradually been slowing in recent months.

## July retail auto sales rebound nicely

After two consecutive months of sluggishness, retail auto sales bounced back in July, with passenger vehicles (PV) and two-wheeler (2W) sales growing 10% and 17%, respectively. However, the symmetrical rebound masks a more heterogenous trend. Given the monthly volatility in auto sales, we step back and look at the six-month moving average (Figure 5). This shows slowing momentum of PV sales in recent months, after their outperformance over the last two years. In contrast, two-wheeler sales had been very soft in the aftermath of the pandemic but have gradually gained momentum, and their growth in recent months has surpassed passenger vehicles. This, alongside a lower demand for MGNREGA — which suggests healing of the rural market — point to a gradual rural recovery, even as urban consumption may be gradually slowing. That said, passenger vehicle sales this year are still 30% above their pre-pandemic levels while two-wheeler sales are 4% below the pre-pandemic trend.

Figure 5: Auto Sales
Vehicle regisrations, %oya 6mma
![](images/8c95f63e6de889a65f9bd2fba943a7807b2a1edac5020c126411e434c2b5ec2b.jpg)
%change 2023-2Source: VAAHAN, J.P. Morgan

## Government capex expectedly slows

Finally, as is often the case for quarters in which general elections are held, government spending saw a sharp decline in the June quarter. Central government capex contracted by a sharp 35% oya (Figure 6) in 2Q while revenues expenditure grew by 2.2%. In the aggregate, central government expenditure contracted by 7.7% last quarter vis-à-vis the same quarter in 2023. The slowdown in capex is showing up in some infrastructure sectors. Cement production slowed to 0.3% oya in 2Q from 7.5% in 1Q, though some of this is on account of base effects. However, steel consumption remained strong last quarter at 14.8% oya. With central government capex budgeted to grow almost 17% this year, we expect central capex to pick up sharply in the coming months to make up for slower spending over the last quarter.

Figure 6: Central government capex
![](images/ac8eeca6e3b8c11148e9006be98f5144da60e8782b37978946d6b014ef91cee9.jpg)

## Explanation of Emerging Markets Sovereign Research Ratings System and Valuation & Methodology:

Ratings System: J.P. Morgan uses the following issuer portfolio weightings for Emerging Markets Sovereign Research: Overweight (over the next three months, the recommended risk position is expected to outperform the relevant index, sector, or benchmark credit returns); Marketweight (over the next three months, the recommended risk position is expected to perform in line with the relevant index, sector, or benchmark credit returns); and Underweight (over the next three months, the recommended risk position is expected to underperform the relevant index, sector, or benchmark credit returns). NR is Not Rated. In this case, J.P. Morgan has removed the rating for this security because of either legal, regulatory or policy reasons or because of lack of a sufficient fundamental basis. The previous rating no longer should be relied upon. An NR designation is not a recommendation or a rating. NC is Not Covered. An NC designation is not a rating or a recommendation. Recommendations will be at the issuer level, and an issuer recommendation applies to all of the index-eligible bonds at the same level for the issuer. When we change the issuer-level rating, we are changing the rating for all of the issues covered, unless otherwise specified. Ratings for quasi-sovereign issuers in the EMBIG may differ from the ratings provided in EM corporate coverage.

Valuation & Methodology: For J.P. Morgan's Emerging Markets Sovereign Research, we assign a rating to each sovereign issuer (Overweight, Marketweight or Underweight) based on our view of whether the combination of the issuer’s fundamentals, market technicals, and the relative value of its securities will cause it to outperform, perform in line with, or underperform the credit returns of the EMBIGD index over the next three months. Our view of an issuer’s fundamentals includes our opinion of whether the issuer is becoming more or less able to service its deb obligations when they become due and payable, as well as whether its willingness to service debt obligations is increasing or decreasing.
